Yet he treads his path undisturbed. Life to all of us is a narrow plank placed across a gulf, which yawns on either side, and if we were perpetually looking down into it we should fall. So at last, the possibility of disaster ceased to affright me. I had been brought off safely so many times when destruction seemed imminent, that I grew hardened, and lay down quietly at night, although the whim of a madman might tomorrow cast me on the pavement. Frequently, as I have said, I could not do this, but I strove to do it, and was able to do it when in health.

I tried to think about nothing which expressed whatever in the world may be insoluble or simply tragic. A great change is just beginning to come over us in this respect. So many books I find are written which aim merely at new presentation of the hopeless. The contradictions of fate, the darkness of death, the fleeting of man over this brief stage of existence, whence we know not, and whither we know not, are favourite subjects with writers who seem to think that they are profound, because they can propose questions which cannot be answered. There is really more strength of mind required for resolving the commonest difficulty than is necessary for the production of poems on these topics. The characteristic of so much that is said and written now is melancholy; and it is melancholy, not because of any deeper acquaintance with the secrets of man than that which was possessed by our forefathers, but because it is easy to be melancholy, and the time lacks strength.

===Chinese===

这时我最凶的敌人是孱弱的身体；因为肩上负有责任，这病痛比原本会更令人愁苦。独居时我知道，自己若有个三长两短，也无甚大不了；可如今每当身上不适，我便为埃伦担忧。她会怎样呢——每当沮丧的恐怖袭来（这是常有的事），这个念头便一夜一夜搅得我合不上眼。不过，随着年岁渐长，那些恐怖已失去了往日的威力。比起从前，我的头脑和神经都安静多了；我也渐渐学会了那门由熟识愁苦而得来的有福的功课：愁苦中骇人的大半在于想象。真正的戈耳工的头，现实中难得一见。我不怀疑它的存在，但它并不像我们以为的那样常见。再者，随着年岁增长，我们发现生命本就是在不确定的条件下交到我们手里的，而我们依然勇敢地走下去。那个苦力结了婚，养了孩子，而横在他与倾覆之间的，只有他自己的力气。每一天都有上百万的偶然迎面而来，其中随便哪一桩都可能叫他残废或丧命，把靠他养活的人送进贫民习艺所。可他照样不为所动地走他的路。对我们所有人来说，生命都是横跨深渊的一块窄板，两侧皆是张开的巨口；倘若我们不停地朝下望，我们就会跌下去。就这样，灾祸的可能性终于不再使我惊惶。多少次毁灭似乎迫在眉睫，我却都安然脱身，于是我心变硬了，夜里安静躺下，尽管说不定明天一个疯子的一时兴到就会把我摔在街石上。如前所说，我常常做不到这样；但我力争做到，而且在身体健康时也确能做到。

我努力不去想世间一切表达无解与纯然悲惨的事物。在这个方面，一场大变化正开始笼罩我们。我发现有那么多的书，其目标不过是对无望之事作一番新的呈现。命运的抵牾、死亡的黑暗、人在生存这座短暂舞台上一掠而过——不知从何处来，亦不知向何处去——这些是作家们偏爱的题目；他们似乎以为，能提出无法回答的问题，便算深刻。其实，解决一桩最寻常的难题，比写这些题目的诗需要更多的心力。当今言谈与文字的通病是忧郁；这忧郁并非因为对人的奥秘比我们的祖先有更深的洞见，而是因为忧郁是件容易事，这个时代缺乏力量。

===Original===

As I am now setting down, without much order or connection, the lessons which I had to learn, I may perhaps be excused if I add one or two others. I can say of them all, that they are not book lessons. They have been taught me by my own experience, and as a rule I have always found that in my own most special perplexities I got but little help from books or other persons. I had to find out for myself what was for me the proper way of dealing with them.

My love for Ellen was great, but I discovered that even such love as this could not be left to itself. It wanted perpetual cherishing. The lamp, if it was to burn brightly, required daily trimming, for people became estranged and indifferent, not so much by open quarrel or serious difference, as by the intervention of trifles which need but the smallest, although continuous effort for their removal. The true wisdom is to waste no time over them, but to eject them at once. Love, too, requires that the two persons who love one another shall constantly present to one another what is best in them, and to accomplish this, deliberate purpose, and even struggle, are necessary. If through relapse into idleness we do not attempt to bring soul and heart into active communion day by day, what wonder if this once exalted relationship become vulgar and mean?

===Chinese===

我此刻记录这些我不得不学的功课，本就缺乏多少条理与连贯，若再添上一两条，想必可以得到谅解。对它们我可以说：没有一条是从书本得来的。教给我它们的是我自己的阅历；而且我一向发现，大凡在我最切身的困惑里，书本和别人给我的帮助都微乎其微。我必须自己去发现，对我来说，应付它们的正当法子是什么。

我对埃伦的爱很深，但我发觉，即便是这样的爱，也不能任其自流。它需要时时刻刻的珍护。灯要烧得亮，就须每日修剪灯芯；人与人变得疏远冷淡，与其说是因为公开的争吵或重大的分歧，不如说是因为种种琐屑之物的介入——清除它们只需最微小、却须持续不断的努力。真正的智慧是不在它们上面耗费工夫，而是立刻把它们请出去。爱也要求两个相爱的人，时时把自己身上最好的那一面呈给对方；要做到这一点，须有自觉的心志，甚至须有挣扎。倘若我们因一时懈怠，不肯日复一日让灵魂与心灵活跃地相通，那么这段曾经崇高的情谊一旦流于庸俗卑微，又有什么奇怪？

===Original===

I was much overworked. It was not the work itself which was such a trial, but the time it consumed. At best, I had but a clear space of an hour, or an hour and a half at home, and to slave merely for this seemed such a mockery! Day after day sped swiftly by, made up of nothing but this infernal drudgery, and I said to myself﻿—Is this life? But I made up my mind that *never would I give myself tongue*. I clapped a muzzle on my mouth. Had I followed my own natural bent, I should have become expressive about what I had to endure, but I found that expression reacts on him who expresses and intensifies what is expressed. If we break out into rhetoric over a toothache, the pangs are not the easier, but the worse to be borne.

I naturally contracted a habit of looking forward from the present moment to one beyond. The whole week seemed to exist for the Sunday. On Monday morning I began counting the hours till Sunday should arrive. The consequence was, that when it came, it was not enjoyed properly, and I wasted it in noting the swiftness of its flight. Oh, how absurd is man! If we were to reckon up all the moments which we really enjoy for their own sake, how few should we find them to be! The greatest part, far the greatest part, of our lives is spent in dreaming over the morrow, and when it comes, it, too, is consumed in the anticipation of a brighter morrow, and so the cheat is prolonged, even to the grave. This tendency, unconquerable though it may appear to be, can to a great extent at any rate, be overcome by strenuous discipline. I tried to blind myself to the future, and many and many a time, as I walked along that dreary New Road or Old St. Pancras Road, have I striven to compel myself not to look at the image of Hampstead Heath or Regent’s Park, as yet six days in front of me, but to get what I could out of what was then with me.

===Chinese===

我劳累过度。难熬的倒不是活计本身，而是它吞掉的时间。至多，我在家里只有一小时或一个半小时的清净光阴，仅仅为了这个去当牛做马，实在像一场嘲弄！一天又一天飞驰而去，塞满了这地狱般的苦役，我对自己说——这难道是生活吗？但我打定了主意：*绝不诉一句苦*。我给嘴上套了个笼头。倘若顺着天性，我早把身受的苦楚四处倾诉了；但我发现，倾诉会反作用于倾诉者，使被倾诉的事愈发沉重。倘若牙疼时我们高声呻吟、夸夸其谈，那疼痛非但不减轻，反而更难忍受。

我自然而然养成了从一个时刻望向下一个时刻的习惯。整一个星期似乎都是为星期日而存在的。星期一早晨我就开始数着钟点，盼星期日到来。结果，星期日真到了，我却没能好好消受，反而把工夫都耗在慨叹它飞逝之疾上。啊，人是多么荒唐！倘若把纯粹因其自身而真正享受的时刻统统算一算，我们会发现它们何其之少！我们一生中最大的一部分——远超一半——都耗在对明日的梦想里；明日来了，又在对更光明的下一个明日的指望中耗尽；这场骗局就这样延续下去，一直延到坟墓。这种倾向看似不可征服，但至少在很大限度内，可以凭严格的操练加以克服。我努力对将来闭起眼睛；多少次，当我走在凄凉的新路（New Road）或旧圣潘克拉斯路（Old St. Pancras Road）上，我强逼自己不去想那还在六天之外的汉普斯特德荒野（Hampstead Heath）或摄政公园（Regent’s Park）的景象，而是从眼前相伴的东西里挤出些滋味来。

===Original===

The instinct which leads us perpetually to compare what we are with what we might be is no doubt of enormous value, and is the spring which prompts all action, but, like every instinct, it is the source of greatest danger. I remember the day and the very spot on which it flashed into me, like a sudden burst of the sun’s rays, that I had no right to this or that﻿—to so much happiness, or even so much virtue. What title-deeds could I show for such a right? Straightway it seemed as if the centre of a whole system of dissatisfaction were removed, and as if the system collapsed. God, creating from His infinite resources a whole infinitude of beings, had created me with a definite position on the scale, and that position only could I claim. Cease the trick of contrast. If I can by any means get myself to consider myself alone without reference to others, discontent will vanish. I walk this Old St. Pancras Road on foot﻿—another rides. Keep out of view him who rides and all persons riding, and I shall not complain that I tramp in the wet. So also when I think how small and weak I am.

How foolish it is to try and cure by argument what time will cure so completely and so gently if left to itself. As I get older, the anxiety to prove myself right if I quarrel dies out. I hold my tongue and time vindicates me, if it is possible to vindicate me, or convicts me if I am wrong. Many and many a debate too which I have had with myself alone has been settled in the same way. The question has been put aside and has lost its importance. The ancient Church thought, and seriously enough, no doubt, that all the vital interests of humanity were bound up with the controversies upon the Divine nature; but the centuries have rolled on, and who cares for those controversies now. The problems of death and immortality once upon a time haunted me so that I could hardly sleep for thinking about them. I cannot tell how, but so it is, that at the present moment, when I am years nearer the end, they trouble me but very little. If I could but bury and let rot things which torment me and come to no settlement﻿—if I could always do this﻿—what a blessing it would be.

===Chinese===

那种引我们不断拿现实之我与可能之我相比的本能，无疑价值巨大，是一切行动的发动之泉；但同一切本能一样，它也是至大危险的源头。我记得那一天、那个地点，这念头如阳光骤然迸射一般照进我心里：我对这个、对那个——对这么多幸福，甚至对这么多德行——都没有权利。这样的权利，我能出示什么契据呢？霎时间，仿佛一整个不满体系的中心被抽掉了，整个体系也随之坍塌。神以其无穷的资源造出无穷的众生，也把我造在阶梯上一个确定的位置；那位置，也只有那位置，是我所能要求的。戒掉对比的把戏吧。只要我能设法单想自己，不参照旁人，不满便会消散。我徒步走这旧圣潘克拉斯路——另一个人坐着车。不去看那坐车的、也不去看一切坐车的人，我便不会抱怨自己在泥水里跋涉。想到自己何等渺小软弱时，也是一样。

想凭论辩去治愈那些交给时间便能痊愈得彻底而温柔的东西，是何等愚蠢。随着年岁增长，争吵之后非要证明自己对的那份焦灼，渐渐消逝了。我闭住嘴，时间自会还我清白——倘若我还清白得可以；我若错了，时间自会定我的罪。还有许许多多我独自一人与自己进行的辩论，也是同样了结的。问题被搁置一边，失掉了分量。古代教会认为——无疑还相当郑重其事——人类一切切身利害都系于关于神性的论战；可几个世纪滚滚而过，如今谁还把那些论战放在心上。死亡与不朽的问题，从前有一阵子纠缠着我，想得我几乎不能入睡。我说不清是怎么一回事，但事实是：此刻，我离终点近了好些年，它们却几乎不再搅扰我。但凡我能把那些折磨我而又了无结论的事物埋掉、任其腐烂——倘若我能永远这样——那将是何等的福气。

## IX Holidays / 假日

===Original===
I have said that Ellen had a child by her first husband. Marie, for that was her name, was now ten years old. She was like neither her mother nor father, and yet was *shot* as it were with strange gleams which reminded me of her paternal grandmother for a moment, and then disappeared. She had rather coarse dark hair, small black eyes, round face, and features somewhat blunt or blurred, the nose in particular being so. She had a tendency to be stout. For books she did not care, and it was with the greatest difficulty we taught her to read. She was not orderly or careful about her person, and in this respect was a sore disappointment—not that she was positively careless, but she took no pride in dress, nor in keeping her room and her wardrobe neat. She was fond of bright colours, which was another trial to Ellen, who disliked any approach to gaudiness. She was not by any means a fool, and she had a peculiarly swift mode of expressing herself upon persons and things. A stranger looking at her would perhaps have adjudged her inclined to sensuousness, and dull. She was neither one nor the other. She ate little, although she was fond of sweets. Her rather heavy face, with no clearly cut outline in it, was not the typical face for passion; but she was capable of passion to an extraordinary degree, and what is more remarkable, it was not explosive passion, or rather it was not passion which she suffered to explode. I remember once when she was a little mite she was asked out somewhere to tea. She was dressed and ready, but it began to rain fast, and she was told she could not go. She besought, but it was in vain. We could not afford cabs, and there was no omnibus. Marie, finding all her entreaties were useless, quietly walked out of the room; and after some little time her mother, calling her and finding she did not come, went to look for her. She had gone into the backyard, and was sitting there in the rain by the side of the water-butt. She was soaked, and her best clothes were spoiled. I must confess that I did not take very kindly to her. I was irritated at her slowness in learning; it was, in fact, painful to be obliged to teach her. I thought that perhaps she might have some undeveloped taste for music, but she showed none, and our attempts to get her to sing ordinary melodies were a failure. She was more or less of a locked cabinet to me. I tried her with the two or three keys which I had, but finding that none of them fitted, I took no more pains about her.

===Chinese===
我说过，埃伦与她前夫有一个孩子。玛丽（Marie）——这是她的名字——如今十岁了。她既不像母亲也不像父亲，却仿佛*织入*了种种奇异的光泽，一时让我想起她的祖母，随即又消失了。她有一头颇粗硬的黑发，小小的黑眼睛，圆脸盘，五官有些钝拙或模糊，鼻子尤其如此。她有发胖的趋势。她对书本毫无兴趣，我们教她识字费尽了九牛二虎之力。她不讲究、也不经心自己的仪容，在这一层上实在令人失望——倒不是说她如何邋遢，而是她对穿戴毫无得意之处，对保持房间和衣橱的整洁也毫不上心。她偏爱鲜亮的颜色，这对埃伦又是一重磨炼——埃伦最不喜欢任何近于俗艳的东西。她绝非愚笨，而且谈论起人和事来，自有一种格外干脆利落的方式。陌生人看她，或许会断定她耽于感官、迟钝乏味。其实她两样都不是。她吃得很少，尽管喜欢甜食。她那张略显得沉重的脸，轮廓不甚分明，本不是容易动情的典型相貌；可她动起情来却能达到非凡的程度，而更值得留意的是，那不是爆烈式的激情——或者不如说，那是她绝不容许爆发出来的激情。我记得她还是个小不点儿的时候，有一次受人邀请外出喝茶。她已穿戴停当，天却下起了急雨，大人告诉她去不成了。她苦苦恳求，终归无用。我们雇不起马车，又没有公共马车可搭。玛丽见恳求一概无效，便一声不响地走出了房间；过了不大一会儿，她母亲喊她，喊不应，便去找她。她走进了后院，坐在雨地里，挨着蓄水桶边上。她浑身湿透，最好的衣裳也毁了。我必须承认，我对她喜欢不起来。她学东西迟钝，惹我恼火；事实上，被迫教她简直是一种苦役。我想她或许藏着某种尚未发端的音乐趣味，可她毫无表现，我们让她唱普通曲调的种种尝试也一败涂地。在我看来，她多少像一口上了锁的柜子。我拿手头仅有的两三把钥匙去试，发现一把也不合，便不再为她费心了。

===Original===
One Sunday we determined upon a holiday. It was a bold adventure for us, but we had made up our minds. There was an excursion train to Hastings, and accordingly Ellen, Marie, and myself were at London Bridge Station early in the morning. It was a lovely summer’s day in mid-July. The journey down was uncomfortable enough in consequence of the heat and dust, but we heeded neither one nor the other in the hope of seeing the sea. We reached Hastings at about eleven o’clock, and strolled westwards towards Bexhill. Our pleasure was exquisite. Who can tell, save the imprisoned Londoner, the joy of walking on the clean sea-sand! What a delight that was, to say nothing of the beauty of the scenery! To be free of the litter and filth of a London suburb, of its broken hedges, its brickbats, its torn advertisements, its worn and trampled grass in fields half given over to the speculative builder: in place of this, to tread the immaculate shore over which breathed a wind not charged with soot; to replace the dull, shrouding obscurity of the smoke by a distance so distinct that the masts of the ships whose hulls were buried below the horizon were visible—all this was perfect bliss. It was not very poetic bliss, perhaps; but nevertheless it is a fact that the cleanness of the sea and the sea air was as attractive to us as any of the sea attributes. We had a wonderful time. Only in the country is it possible to note the change of morning into midday, of midday into afternoon, and of afternoon into evening; and it is only in the country, therefore, that a day seems stretched out into its proper length. We had brought all our food with us, and sat upon the shore in the shadow of a piece of the cliff. A row of heavy white clouds lay along the horizon almost unchangeable and immovable, with their summit-lines and the part of the mass just below them steeped in sunlight. The level opaline water differed only from a floor by a scarcely perceptible heaving motion, which broke into the faintest of ripples at our feet. So still was the great ocean, so quietly did everything lie in it, that the wavelets which licked the beach were as pure and bright as if they were a part of the mid-ocean depths. About a mile from us, at one o’clock, a long row of porpoises appeared, showing themselves in graceful curves for half-an-hour or so, till they went out farther to sea off Fairlight. Some fishing-boats were becalmed just in front of us. Their shadows slept, or almost slept, upon the water, a gentle quivering alone showing that it was not complete sleep, or if sleep, that it was sleep with dreams. The intensity of the sunlight sharpened the outlines of every little piece of rock, and of the pebbles, in a manner which seemed supernatural to us Londoners. In London we get the heat of the sun, but not his light, and the separation of individual parts into such vivid isolation was so surprising that even Marie noticed it, and said it “all seemed as if she were looking through a glass.” It was perfect—perfect in its beauty—and perfect because, from the sun in the heavens down to the fly with burnished wings on the hot rock, there was nothing out of harmony. Everything breathed one spirit. Marie played near us; Ellen and I sat still, doing nothing. We wanted nothing, we had nothing to achieve; there were no curiosities to be seen, there was no particular place to be reached, no “plan of operations,” and London was forgotten for the time. It lay behind us in the northwest, and the cliff was at our back shutting out all thought of it. No reminiscences and no anticipations disturbed us; the present was sufficient, and occupied us totally.

===Chinese===
一个星期天，我们决定去度一天假。这于我们是一场大胆的冒险，但我们心意已决。有一班开往黑斯廷斯（Hastings）的专列，于是埃伦、玛丽和我一清早便到了伦敦桥车站（London Bridge Station）。那是七月中旬一个明媚的夏日。一路酷热与尘土，行程够难受的，但怀着看海的指望，这两样我们都不放在心上。约莫十一点我们抵达黑斯廷斯，信步向西往贝克斯希尔（Bexhill）而去。我们的快乐难以言表。除了被囚禁的伦敦人，谁能道出走在洁净海沙上的欢愉！这已是何等欢畅，更不必说风景之美！逃离伦敦郊区的垃圾与污秽——那残破的篱笆、碎砖烂瓦、撕破的广告、半数已让投机营造商侵占的田野里被踩得稀烂的草地——代之以踏上纤尘不染的海岸，海上吹来的风不含一粒煤烟；代之以用一片如此清晰渺远的天际，替换烟雾那沉闷的、裹尸布似的昏晦——清晰得连船身隐没在地平线之下的那些船只的桅杆都历历可见——这一切是十全的至福。这或许算不上很有诗意的至福；然而事实是，海与海风的洁净，同海洋的任何特质一样吸引着我们。我们度过了一段美妙的时光。唯有在乡野，才观察得出清晨如何变成正午、正午如何变成午后、午后又如何变成黄昏；因此，也唯有在乡野，一天才似乎舒展到它本来的长度。我们随身带足了食物，坐在海岸上一段峭壁的阴影里。一长列厚重的白云横卧在天际，几乎凝然不动，云顶的轮廓和云体紧贴其下的部分都浸透了阳光。那平铺的、蛋白色的海面，同一面地板的差别只在微微可察的起伏，到了我们脚边，才碎成最细弱的涟漪。大海是这样宁静，万物这样安然卧于其中，舔着沙滩的细浪纯净明亮，仿佛是大洋腹地的一部分。一点钟左右，离我们约一英里的海面上出现了一长列鼠海豚，以优雅的弧线翻腾了约莫半个小时，直到向费尔莱特（Fairlight）外更远的海面游去。几艘渔船风息帆垂，正泊在我们跟前。船影睡在水上，或者说几乎睡了，只有一阵轻轻的颤动表明那不是酣睡；纵然是睡，也是带着梦的睡。阳光的炽烈把每一小块岩石、每一粒卵石的轮廓都磨砺得锋芒毕露，在我们这些伦敦人看来几近超自然。在伦敦，我们得到太阳的热，却得不到它的光；各个个体被析离得如此鲜明夺目，令人惊讶之极，连玛丽也注意到了，说这一切「看上去就像隔着一面玻璃在看」。这是圆满的——美得圆满——而且圆满，因为从天上的太阳直到灼热岩石上那翅膀闪亮的苍蝇，没有一样东西失于调和。万物吐纳着同一个灵。玛丽在我们近旁玩耍；埃伦和我静静坐着，无所事事。我们一无所求，也无可成就；没有奇珍可看，没有特定的地方要赶，没有「行动计划」，伦敦一时被忘了个干净。它卧在我们身后的西北方，峭壁挡在我们背后，隔断了对它的一切念想。没有回忆，也没有期待来搅扰我们；当下已经足够，把我们整个占据了。

===Original===
I should like, if I could, to write an essay upon the art of enjoying a holiday. It is sad to think how few people know how to enjoy one, although they are so precious. We do not sufficiently consider that enjoyment of every kind is an art carefully to be learnt, and specially the art of making the most of a brief space set apart for pleasure. It is foolish, for example, if a man, city bred, has but twelve hours before him, to spend more of it in eating and drinking than is necessary. Eating and drinking produce stupidity, at least in some degree, which may just as well be reserved for town. It is foolish also to load the twelve hours with a task—so much to be done. The sick person may perhaps want exercise, but to the tolerably healthy the best of all recreation is the freedom from fetters even when they are self-imposed.

===Chinese===
倘若力所能及，我很想写一篇文章，论一论享受假日的艺术。假日如此珍贵，却很少有人懂得如何享受，想起来令人悲哀。我们不曾充分想到：一切享乐都是一门需要用心修习的艺术，尤其是把短短一段专供愉悦的时光用到极致的艺术。譬如，一个城里长大的人，面前只有十二个钟头，却在饮食上花费超出必要的工夫，那就是愚蠢。饮食至少会在某种程度上制造愚钝，而这种愚钝大可留到城里去受用。把十二个钟头装满一堆任务——要做这么多这么多事——同样是愚蠢。病人或许需要运动，但对相当健康的人来说，一切消遣中之最佳者，是挣脱镣铐的自由——哪怕那镣铐是自己锻造的。

===Original===
Our train homewards was due at Bexhill a little after seven. By five o’clock a change gradual but swift was observed. The clouds which had charmed us all through the morning and afternoon were in reality thunderclouds, which woke up like a surprised army under perfect discipline, and moved magnificently towards us. Already afar off we heard the softened echoing roll of the thunder. Every now and then we saw a sharp thrust of lightning down into the water, and shuddered when we thought that perhaps underneath that stab there might be a ship with living men. The battle at first was at such a distance that we watched it with intense and solemn delight. As yet not a breath of air stirred, but presently, over in the southeast, a dark ruffled patch appeared on the horizon, and we agreed that it was time to go. The indistinguishable continuous growl now became articulated into distinct crashes. I had miscalculated the distance to the station, and before we got there the rain, skirmishing in advance, was upon us. We took shelter in a cottage for a moment in order that Ellen might get a glass of water—bad-looking stuff it was, but she was very thirsty—and put on her cloak. We then started again on our way. We reached the station at about half-past six, before the thunder was overhead, but not before Ellen had got wet, despite all my efforts to protect her. She was also very hot from hurrying, and yet there was nothing to be done but to sit in a kind of covered shed till the train came up. The thunder and lightning were, however, so tremendous, that we thought of nothing else. When they were at their worst, the lightning looked like the upset of a cauldron of white glowing metal—with such strength, breadth, and volume did it descend. Just as the train arrived, the roar began to abate, and in about half-an-hour it had passed over to the north, leaving behind the rain, cold and continuous, which fell all round us from a dark, heavy, grey sky. The carnage in which we were was a third-class, with seats arranged parallel to the sides. It was crowded, and we were obliged to sit in the middle, exposed to the draught which the tobacco smoke made necessary. Some of the company were noisy, and before we got to Red Hill became noisier, as the brandy-flasks which had been well filled at Hastings began to work. Many were drenched, and this was an excuse for much of the drinking; although for that matter, any excuse or none is generally sufficient. At Red Hill we were stopped by other trains, and before we came to Croydon we were an hour late. We had now become intolerably weary. The songs were disgusting, and some of the women who were with the men had also been drinking, and behaved in a manner which it was not pleasant that Ellen and Marie should see. The carriage was lighted fortunately by one dim lamp only which hung in the middle, and I succeeded at last in getting seats at the further end, where there was a knot of more decent persons who had huddled up there away from the others. All the glory of the morning was forgotten. Instead of three happy, exalted creatures, we were three dejected, shivering mortals, half poisoned with foul air and the smell of spirits. We crawled up to London Bridge at the slowest pace, and, finally, the railway company discharged us on the platform at ten minutes past eleven. Not a place in any omnibus could be secured, and we therefore walked for a mile or so till I saw a cab, which—unheard-of expense for me—I engaged, and we were landed at our own house exactly at half-past twelve. The first thing to be done was to get Marie to bed. She was instantly asleep, and was none the worse for her journey. With Ellen the case was different. She could not sleep, and the next morning was feverish. She insisted that it was nothing more than a bad cold, and would on no account permit me even to give her any medicine. She would get up presently, and she and Marie could get on well enough together. But when I reached home on Monday evening, Ellen was worse, and was still in bed.

===Chinese===
我们回程的火车定于七点刚过到达贝克斯希尔。到了五点钟，一场渐进却迅疾的变化显现了。整个上午和下午令我们心醉的那些云，原来是雷雨云；它们像一支纪律严明的军队从惊愕中醒来，堂堂之阵向我们压来。远处已经传来隐隐滚动的雷声。不时有一道闪电凌厉地刺入水中，我们一想到那一刺之下或许正有一艘载着活人的船，便不寒而栗。战事起初尚在遥远之处，我们怀着紧张而肃穆的欢欣观看着。此时还一丝风也没有，但不一会儿，东南方的天际出现了一块吹皱的黑色斑痕，我们一致认为该走了。先前浑然一片的连绵低吼，此刻已吐字成一声声分明的炸裂。我把到车站的路程估错了，还没走到，打前站的雨已经扑上身来。我们在一所村舍里躲了片刻，好让埃伦喝一杯水——那水瞧着不像样，可她实在渴极了——再披上斗篷。随后我们重新上路。约莫六点半到了车站，雷声还没到头顶，埃伦却已经淋湿了，尽管我千方百计护着她。她又因赶路而浑身燥热，可别无他法，只能坐在一种带顶棚的棚子里等火车进站。然而雷声电光声势惊人，我们别的什么都顾不上想了。在最猛烈的时候，那闪电好似一锅白热熔金的倾翻——它倾泻而下，那样猛，那样阔，那样厚。火车刚到，雷吼便开始衰减，约莫半个钟头后，它已移到北面，只留下又冷又不停的雨，从阴暗沉重的灰天上下向我们四周。我们所在的那节车厢——堪称一间「屠场」——是三等车厢，座位顺着车壁平行排列。车厢里挤满了人，我们只得坐在当中，任凭穿堂风吹——抽烟的人使得通风成了必需。同车的一些人吵吵嚷嚷，还没到雷德希尔（Red Hill）就嚷得更凶了，因为在黑斯廷斯灌得满满的白兰地酒瓶开始发作。许多人浑身透湿，这成了大量灌酒的理由；不过说到这个，有理由也好没理由也好，通常都足够了。在雷德希尔我们被别的火车挡住，还没到克罗伊登（Croydon）就已经晚了一个钟头。我们这时已困顿得难以忍受。那些歌声污秽不堪，随男人们同行的几个女人也喝了酒，举止之状不宜让埃伦和玛丽看见。所幸车厢只靠中间悬着的一盏昏灯照明，我总算挪到了车厢另一头的座位，那里蜷着一小群较为体面的人，躲开了其余的人。清晨全部的荣光都被忘却了。我们不再是三个幸福而昂扬的人，而是三个沮丧发抖的凡夫，被浊空气和酒气熏得半醉。我们以最慢的速度爬到伦敦桥，最终，铁路公司在十一点十分把我们卸在站台上。任何一辆公共马车里都觅不到座位，我们只好步行了一英里上下，直到我看见一辆出租马车——这于我是闻所未闻的奢侈——雇下了它，十二点半整，我们被送到了自家门口。头一件事是把玛丽送上床。她倒头便睡，旅途于她毫无损伤。埃伦的情形却不同。她无法入睡，第二天早晨便发起烧来。她一口咬定不过是一场重感冒，说什么也不肯让我给她吃药。她很快就会起来的，她和玛丽两个在一起尽可以过得去。可到星期一傍晚我回到家时，埃伦更重了，还卧在床上。

===Original===
I sent at once for the doctor, who would give no opinion for a day or two, but meanwhile directed that she was to remain where she was, and take nothing but the lightest food. Tuesday night passed, and the fever still increased. I had become very anxious, but I dared not stay with her, for I knew not what might happen if I were absent from my work. I was obliged to try and think of somebody who would come and help us. Our friend Taylor, who once was the coal-porter at Somerset House, came into my mind. He, as I have said when talking about him, was married, but had no children. To him accordingly I went. I never shall forget the alacrity with which he prompted his wife to go, and with which she consented. I was shut up in my own sufferings, but I remember a flash of joy that all our efforts in our room had not been in vain. I was delighted that I had secured assistance, but I do believe the uppermost thought was delight that we had been able to develop gratitude and affection. Mrs. Taylor was an “ordinary woman.” She was about fifty, rather stout, and entirely uneducated. But when she took charge at our house, all her best qualities found expression. It is true enough, *omnium consensu capax imperii nisi imperasset*, but it is equally true that under the pressure of trial and responsibility we are often stronger than when there is no pressure. Many a man will acknowledge that in difficulty he has surprised himself by a resource and coolness which he never suspected before. Mrs. Taylor I always thought to be rather weak and untrustworthy, but I found that when *weight* was placed upon her, she was steady as a rock, a systematic and a perfect manager. There was no doubt in a very short time as to the nature of the disease. It was typhoid fever, the cause probably being the impure water drunk as we were coming home. I have no mind to describe what Ellen suffered. Suffice it to say, that her treatment was soon reduced to watching her every minute night and day, and administering small quantities of milk. Her prostration and emaciation were excessive, and without the most constant attention she might at any moment have slipped out of our hands. I was like a man shipwrecked and alone in a polar country, whose existence depends upon one spark of fire, which he tries to cherish, left glimmering in a handful of ashes. Oh those days, prolonged to weeks, during which that dreadful struggle lasted—days swallowed up with one sole, intense, hungry desire that her life might be spared!—days filled with a forecast of the blackness and despair before me if she should depart. I tried to obtain release from the office. The answer was that nobody could of course prevent my being away, but that it was not usual for a clerk to be absent merely because his wife was not well. The brute added with a sneer that a wife was “a luxury” which he should have thought I could hardly afford. We divided between us, however, at home the twenty-four hours during which we stood sentinels against death, and occasionally we were relieved by one or two friends. I went on duty from about eight in the evening till one in the morning, and was then relieved by Mrs. Taylor, who remained till ten or eleven. She then went to bed, and was replaced by little Marie. What a change came over that child! I was amazed at her. All at once she seemed to have found what she was born to do. The key had been discovered, which unlocked and revealed what there was in her, of which hitherto I had been altogether unaware. Although she was so little, she became a perfect nurse. Her levity disappeared; she was grave as a matron, moved about as if shod in felt, never forgot a single direction, and gave proper and womanly answers to strangers who called. Faculties unsuspected grew almost to full height in a single day. Never did she relax during the whole of that dreadful time, or show the slightest sign of discontent. She sat by her mother’s side, intent, vigilant; and she had her little dinner prepared and taken up into the sickroom by Mrs. Taylor before she went to bed. I remember once going to her cot in the night, as she lay asleep, and almost breaking my heart over her with remorse and thankfulness—remorse, that I, with blundering stupidity, had judged her so superficially; and thankfulness, that it had pleased God to present to me so much of His own divinest grace. Fool that I was, not to be aware that messages from Him are not to be read through the envelope in which they are enclosed. I never should have believed, if it had not been for Marie, that any grown-up man could so love a child. Such love, I should have said, was only possible between man and woman, or, perhaps, between man and man. But now I doubt whether a love of that particular kind could be felt towards any grown-up human being, love so pure, so imperious, so awful. My love to Marie was love of God Himself as He is—an unrestrained adoration of an efflux from Him, adoration transfigured into love, because the revelation had clothed itself with a child’s form. It was, as I say, the love of God as He is. It was not necessary, as it so often is necessary, to qualify, to subtract, to consider the other side, to deplore the obscurity or the earthly contamination with which the Word is delivered to us. This was the Word itself, without even consciousness on the part of the instrument selected for its vocalisation. I may appear extravagant, but I can only put down what I felt and still feel. I appeal, moreover, to Jesus Himself for justification. I had seen the kingdom of God through a little child. I, in fact, have done nothing more than beat out over a page in my own words what passed through His mind when He called a little child and set him in the midst of His disciples. How I see the meaning of those words now! and so it is that a text will be with us for half a lifetime, recognised as great and good, but not penetrated till the experience comes round to us in which it was born.

===Chinese===
我立刻去请医生。医生一两天内不肯下断语，只嘱咐她原地静养，饮食唯取最清淡之物。星期二夜里过去了，热度还在攀升。我已忧心如焚，却不敢守在她身边，因为倘若我旷了工，天知道会发生什么。我只得设法想起一个肯来帮助我们的人。我们的朋友泰勒——当年在萨默塞特府（Somerset House）当过运煤工的那位——浮上我的心头。我说过，他成了婚，没有孩子。我便去找他。我永远忘不了他催促妻子前往时的那份爽利，也忘不了她一口应承时的爽利。我把自己关在自己的苦楚里，可我记得那一闪的欢欣：我们在小屋里的一切辛劳并没有白费。我高兴寻到了帮手，但我确信，最居上风的念头是欢喜：我们竟能培育出感恩与爱。泰勒太太是位「寻常女人」。她五十上下，身子发胖，全然没有受过教育。可她一到我们家掌管事务，她全部最好的品质便都找到了表达。*omnium consensu capax imperii nisi imperasset*（人人公认他有御世之才——倘若他不曾御世的话），这话固然不假；但同样真实的是，在试炼与责任的压力之下，我们往往比没有压力时更坚强。许多人都会承认，身处困境时，他曾以自己从未料到的机变与镇定让自己吃惊。我素来觉得泰勒太太相当软弱、靠不住，可我发现，一旦把*分量*压在她身上，她便稳如磐石，是个有条有理、无可挑剔的管家。不久，病的性质便再无怀疑了。是伤寒，病因多半是回家路上喝的那口不洁之水。我没有心思去描写埃伦受了怎样的苦。只需说：她的治疗很快就缩减为日夜每一分钟守着她，和喂她少量的牛奶。她的虚脱与羸瘦到了极点，若非最不间断的看护，她随时可能从我们手中滑走。我像一个在极地失事孤存的人，性命系于一星火种，他百般呵护，只余一把灰烬里的微光。哦，那些拖延成数周的日子，那场可怕的搏斗贯穿其间——那些日子被一个唯一的、炽烈的、如饥似渴的愿望吞没：但愿她的性命得以保全！——那些日子充满了预感：她若去了，我面前的黑暗与绝望将是怎样。我设法向办公所告假。答复是：当然没有人能拦着我离开，但一个小职员仅仅因为妻子欠安便告假，未免不合常例。那畜生还冷笑着补了一句，说妻子是一种「奢侈品」，他原以为我未必供养得起。然而我们在家中分守那与死亡对峙的二十四小时，偶尔有一两位朋友来替我们一两个班。我当班从晚上八点左右到凌晨一点，然后由泰勒太太接班，守到十点或十一点。她随后上床就寝，换小玛丽来接。那孩子身上发生了怎样的变化啊！我为之惊异。她仿佛一下子找到了自己天生该做之事。那把钥匙被发现了，它打开并照亮了她里面的东西——那是我此前全然不知的。她年纪虽小，却成了一名十全十美的看护。她的轻浮消失了；她庄重得像位主妇，走动时仿佛脚下踩着毡底，一条嘱咐也不曾忘记，对登门的陌生人应答得体、俨然大人。未曾疑及的才具，几乎在一日之间长到了近于全高。在那整个可怕的日子里，她从不松懈，也从不露出一丝一毫不情愿。她坐在母亲身旁，专注而警醒；她的小晚饭先请泰勒太太备好，在她就寝前送进病房。我记得有一天夜里走到她的小床边，看她睡熟在那里，悔恨与感激几乎令我心碎——悔恨的是，我竟以笨伯的愚钝，如此浅薄地评判过她；感激的是，神竟乐意把祂自己至圣的恩典，这样多地呈现在我面前。我真是个傻瓜，竟不知晓：从祂而来的信息，原不能隔着封装它的信封去读。若不是玛丽，我绝不会相信一个成年男子竟能这样爱一个孩子。我本会说，这样的爱只可能存在于男人与女人之间，或者，存在于男人与男人之间。可如今我怀疑，这种特殊的爱能否施于任何一个成年人——这样纯净、这样不容抗拒、这样令人敬畏的爱。我对玛丽的爱，是对神本身如其所是的爱——是对祂一道流溢的无遮无拦的崇仰；这崇仰化成了爱，因为那启示为自己披上了一个孩子的形貌。正如我所说，这是对神本身如其所是的爱。它无须——像如此经常地不可避免的那样——打折扣、做减损、考虑另一面、为圣道交付我们时所裹的晦暗或尘俗的沾染而悲叹。这乃是圣道本身，甚至那被拣选来发声的器皿也全无自觉。我也许显得夸张，可我只能写下我当时感到、至今仍感到的东西。况且，我还向耶稣本人请求公证。我已借着一个小孩看见了神的国。事实上，我不过是用我自己的话，在纸页上敲打了当祂唤来一个小孩、把他安置在门徒中间时，掠过祂心中的所思。如今我多么看清了那些话的含义啊！一节经文与我们相伴半生，被公认为伟大而良善，却要等到诞生它的那种经历轮转到我们身上时，才被参透——事情就是这样。

===Original===
Six weeks passed before the faint blue point of light which flickered on the wick began to turn white and show some strength. At last, however, day by day, we marked a slight accession of vitality which increased with change of diet. Every evening when I came home I was gladdened by the tidings which showed advance, and Ellen, I believe, was as much pleased to see how others rejoiced over her recovery as she was pleased for her own sake. She, too, was one of those creatures who always generously admit improvement. For my own part, I have often noticed that when I have been ill, and have been getting better, I have refused to acknowledge it, and that it has been an effort to me to say that things were not at their worst. She, however, had none of this niggardly baseness, and always, if only for the sake of her friends, took the cheerful side. Mrs. Taylor now left us. She left us a friend whose friendship will last, I hope, as long as life lasts. She had seen all our troubles and our poverty: we knew that she knew all about us: she had helped us with the most precious help—what more was there necessary to knit her to us?—and it is worth noting that the assistance which she rendered, and her noble self-sacrifice, so far from putting us, in her opinion, in her debt, only seemed to her a reason why she should be more deeply attached to us.

===Chinese===
六个星期过去，灯芯上摇曳的那一点微弱的蓝光才开始转白、显出些力气。然而终于，我们一天一天地看出生机略有增益，并随着饮食的更换而增长。每天傍晚我回到家，显示好转的消息都令我欢喜；而我相信，埃伦看见别人为她的康复如何欢欣，那份快慰不亚于为她自己的缘故。她也属于那样一种人：总是大方地承认好转。就我自己而论，我常常注意到，我生病见好时总是不肯承认，要我说一句「事情不是在最坏处」，也得费一番气力。她却没有这种吝啬的卑劣，总是——哪怕只为了朋友的缘故——拣光明的一面。泰勒太太如今离开了我们。她留给我们的是一位朋友，我希望这份情谊与生命同长久。她见过了我们全部的苦难与贫穷：我们知道她对我们的一切了如指掌：她以最珍贵的帮助相助——还要什么来把她与我们系在一起呢？——而且值得记下一笔：她所给予的援手、她高贵的自我牺牲，在她看来非但没有使我们欠她的债，反倒只成了她更深深依恋我们的一个理由。

===Original===
It was late in the autumn before Ellen had thoroughly recovered, but at last we said that she was as strong as she was before, and we determined to celebrate our deliverance by one more holiday before the cold weather came. It was again Sunday—a perfectly still, warm, autumnal day, with a high barometer and the gentlest of airs from the west. The morning in London was foggy, so much so that we doubted at first whether we should go; but my long experience of London fog told me that we should escape from it with that wind if we got to the chalk downs away out by Letherhead and Guildford. We took the early train to a point at the base of the hills, and wound our way up into the woods at the top. We were beyond the smoke, which rested like a low black cloud over the city in the northeast, reaching a third of the way up to the zenith. The beech had changed colour, and glowed with reddish-brown fire. We sat down on a floor made of the leaves of last year. At midday the stillness was profound, broken only by the softest of whispers descending from the great trees which spread over us their protecting arms. Every now and then it died down almost to nothing, and then slowly swelled and died again, as if the Gods of the place were engaged in divine and harmonious talk. By moving a little towards the external edge of our canopy we beheld the plain all spread out before us, bounded by the heights of Sussex and Hampshire. It was veiled with the most tender blue, and above it was spread a sky which was white on the horizon and deepened by degrees into azure over our heads. The exhilaration of the air satisfied Marie, although she had no playmate, and there was nothing special with which she could amuse herself. She wandered about looking for flowers and ferns, and was content. We were all completely happy. We strained our eyes to see the furthest point before us, and we tried to find it on the map we had brought with us. The season of the year, which is usually supposed to make men pensive, had no such effect upon us. Everything in the future, even the winter in London, was painted by Hope, and the death of the summer brought no sadness. Rather did summer dying in such fashion fill our hearts with repose, and even more than repose—with actual joy.

===Chinese===
入秋很深，埃伦才彻底复原，但我们终于可以说她已强健如初，便决定趁严寒未至，再过一个假日，以庆我们的解脱。又是星期天——一个全然静谧、温暖的秋日，气压很高，西面吹来至柔的风。伦敦的早晨有雾，浓得我们起初还犹豫去不去；但我对伦敦雾的长年经验告诉我，乘着这样的风，只要到莱瑟黑德（Letherhead）和吉尔福德（Guildford）以外那片白垩丘陵去，便能逃出雾外。我们乘早班火车到山脚下，盘旋而上，走进山顶的树林。我们已身在烟霭之外；那烟像一朵低垂的黑云覆在东北方的城市上空，直抵天顶三分之一的高度。山毛榉已换了颜色，燃着赤褐的火。我们坐在一层去年落叶铺成的地板上。正午时分，寂静深沉，只有最柔软的絮语从大树顶上飘落下来打断它——那些大树向我们张开了庇护的手臂。絮语时时低回，几近于无，然后又缓缓涌起，再归于沉寂，仿佛此地的诸神正进行着神圣而和谐的谈话。向我们的华盖外缘挪动几步，便望见平原尽铺眼前，以苏塞克斯（Sussex）和汉普郡（Hampshire）的高地为界。平原罩着最柔嫩的蓝，其上展开的天空，天际泛白，渐次加深，到我们头顶已成碧蓝。空气的爽劲使玛丽心满意足，尽管她没有玩伴，也没有什么特别的玩意儿可自娱。她四处游走，寻花觅蕨，自得其乐。我们全都全然地幸福。我们极目远望眼前最远的一点，又试着在我们随身带的地图上把它找出来。一年中的这个时节，通常被认为令人沉思，在我们身上却全无此效。未来的一切，连伦敦的冬天在内，都被希望着上了色；夏天的死亡没有带来悲哀。夏天这样地死去，反倒使我们心中充满安宁，且不止于安宁——充满实实在在的欢欣。

---

===Original===
Here ends the autobiography. A month after this last holiday my friend was dead and buried. He had unsuspected disease of the heart, and one day his master, of whom we have heard something, was more than usually violent. Mark, as his custom was, was silent, but evidently greatly excited. His tyrant left the room; and in a few minutes afterwards Mark was seen to turn white and fall forward in his chair. It was all over! His body was taken to a hospital and thence sent home. The next morning his salary up to the day of his death came in an envelope to his widow, without a single word from his employers save a request for acknowledgment. Towards midday, his office coat, and a book found in his drawer, arrived in a brown paper parcel, carriage unpaid.

On looking over his papers, I found the sketch of his life and a mass of odds and ends, some apparently written for publication. Many of these had evidently been in envelopes, and had most likely, therefore, been offered to editors or publishers, but all, I am sure, had been refused. I add one or two by way of appendix, and hope they will be thought worth saving.

R. S.

===Chinese===
自传至此为止。这最后一次度假之后一个月，我的朋友便死去下葬了。他患有未曾察觉的心疾。有一天，他那上司——关于此人我们已有所闻——异乎寻常地暴戾。马克（Mark）照他的习惯沉默着，但显然激愤已极。那暴君离开了房间；几分钟后，人们看见马克脸色变白，向前栽倒在椅子里。一切都完了！他的遗体被送往一家医院，又从那里运回家中。第二天早晨，他截至死亡当日的薪水装在一只信封里送到他的遗孀手中，雇主方面没有一句话，只要求签收。临近中午，他的办公外套，还有从他抽屉里找到的一本书，装在一只棕色纸包裹里送到，运费未付。

翻检他的文稿时，我发现了那份生平记述和一大堆零散文字，有些显然是为发表而写的。其中许多分明装过信封，因此多半曾投给过编辑或出版商，但我确信全都被退回了。我附上一两篇作为附录，希望它们会被认为值得保存。

R. S.

## II M’Kay / II 麦凯

===Original===

It was foggy and overcast as we walked home to Goodge Street. The churches and chapels were emptying themselves, but the great mass of the population had been “nowhere.” I had dinner with M’Kay, and as the day wore on the fog thickened. London on a dark Sunday afternoon, more especially about Goodge Street, is depressing. The inhabitants drag themselves hither and thither in languor and uncertainty. Small mobs loiter at the doors of the gin palaces. Costermongers wander aimlessly, calling “walnuts” with a cry so melancholy that it sounds as the wail of the hopelessly lost may be imagined to sound when their anguish has been deadened by the monotony of a million years.

About two or three o’clock decent working men in their best clothes emerge from the houses in such streets as Nassau Street. It is part of their duty to go out after dinner on Sunday with the wife and children. The husband pushes the perambulator out of the dingy passage, and gazes doubtfully this way and that way, not knowing whither to go, and evidently longing for the Monday, when his work, however disagreeable it may be, will be his plain duty. The wife follows carrying a child, and a boy and girl in unaccustomed apparel walk by her side. They come out into Mortimer Street. There are no shops open; the sky over their heads is mud, the earth is mud under their feet, the muddy houses stretch in long rows, black, gaunt, uniform. The little party reach Hyde Park, also wrapped in impenetrable mud-grey. The man’s face brightens for a moment as he says, “It is time to go back,” and so they return, without the interchange of a word, unless perhaps they happen to see an omnibus horse fall down on the greasy stones. What is there worth thought or speech on such an expedition? Nothing! The tradesman who kept the oil and colour establishment opposite to us was not to be tempted outside. It was a little more comfortable than Nassau Street, and, moreover, he was religious and did not encourage Sabbath-breaking. He and his family always moved after their midday Sabbath repast from the little back room behind the shop up to what they called the drawing-room overhead. It was impossible to avoid seeing them every time we went to the window. The father of the family, after his heavy meal, invariably sat in the easy-chair with a handkerchief over his eyes and slept. The children were always at the windows, pretending to read books, but in reality watching the people below. At about four o’clock their papa generally awoke, and demanded a succession of hymn tunes played on the piano. When the weather permitted, the lower sash was opened a little, and the neighbours were indulged with the performance of “Vital Spark,” the father “coming in” now and then with a bass note or two at the end where he was tolerably certain of the harmony. At five o’clock a prophecy of the incoming tea brought us some relief from the contemplation of the landscape or brick-scape. I say “some relief,” for meals at M’Kay’s were a little disagreeable. His wife was an honest, good little woman, but so much attached to him and so dependent on him that she was his mere echo. She had no opinions which were not his, and whenever he said anything which went beyond the ordinary affairs of the house, she listened with curious effort, and generally responded by a weakened repetition of M’Kay’s own observations. He perpetually, therefore, had before him an enfeebled reflection of himself, and this much irritated him, notwithstanding his love for her; for who could help loving a woman who, without the least hesitation, would have opened her veins at his command, and have given up every drop of blood in her body for him? Over and over again I have heard him offer some criticism on a person or event, and the customary chime of approval would ensue, provoking him to such a degree that he would instantly contradict himself with much bitterness, leaving poor Mrs. M’Kay in much perplexity. Such a shot as this generally reduced her to timid silence. As a rule, he always discouraged any topic at his house which was likely to serve as an occasion for showing his wife’s dependence on him. He designedly talked about her household affairs, asked her whether she had mended his clothes and ordered the coals. She knew that these things were not what was upon his mind, and she answered him in despairing tones, which showed how much she felt the obtrusive condescension to her level. I greatly pitied her, and sometimes, in fact, my emotion at the sight of her struggles with her limitations almost overcame me and I was obliged to get up and go. She was childishly affectionate. If M’Kay came in and happened to go up to her and kiss her, her face brightened into the sweetest and happiest smile. I recollect once after he had been unusually annoyed with her he repented just as he was leaving home, and put his lips to her head, holding it in both his hands. I saw her gently take the hand from her forehead and press it to her mouth, the tears falling down her cheek meanwhile. Nothing would ever tempt her to admit anything against her husband. M’Kay was violent and unjust at times. His occupation he hated, and his restless repugnance to it frequently discharged itself indifferently upon everything which came in his way. His children often thought him almost barbarous, but in truth he did not actually see them when he was in one of these moods. What was really present with him, excluding everything else, was the sting of something more than usually repulsive of which they knew nothing. Mrs. M’Kay’s answer to her children’s remonstrances when they were alone with her always was, “He is so worried,” and she invariably dwelt upon their faults which had given him the opportunity for his wrath.

===Chinese===

我们走回古奇街（Goodge Street）的家时，天色雾蒙蒙、阴沉沉。教堂和礼拜堂正在散场，但大多数人都“哪儿也没去”。我在麦凯（M’Kay）家吃的饭；随着天色渐晚，雾越来越浓。伦敦在昏暗的星期日下午，尤其在古奇街一带，是叫人沮丧的。居民们无精打采、无所适从地拖着步子东游西荡。一小群一小群的人聚在杜松子酒肆门口闲逛。小贩们漫无目的地游走，叫卖着“核桃”，那叫声如此凄凉，仿佛永世沉沦者的哀号——他们的痛苦已被一百万年的单调磨得麻木——在我们想象中该有的那种声音。

两三点钟光景，正派的工人们穿上最好的衣裳，从拿骚街（Nassau Street）一带的各户人家走出来。星期日饭后带着妻儿出门走走，是他们的一项义务。丈夫把婴儿车推出昏暗的过道，犹疑地东张西望，不知该往哪里去，心里显然盼着星期一，到那时他的活计无论多么不顺心，至少是他明明白白的本分。妻子抱着一个孩子跟在后面，一儿一女穿着不常上身的新衣，走在她身旁。他们来到莫蒂默街（Mortimer Street）。店铺全都闭着门；头顶的天是泥浆色，脚下的地是泥浆色，泥浆色的房屋一排排伸展开去，黑黢黢、瘦棱棱，千篇一律。这一小队人马到了海德公园（Hyde Park），公园也裹在透不进光的泥灰色里。“该回去了。”男人说这句话时，脸上亮了一瞬。于是他们往回走，一路再不交一语，除非碰巧看见一匹公共马车的马滑倒在油污的石块上。这样一趟出游，有什么值得想、值得说的呢？没有！我们对门开油彩铺子的那位店主，是不肯被诱出门的。他家比拿骚街的人家稍微舒适些，况且他虔信宗教，不赞成在安息日游乐。每逢安息日午饭后，他一家总是从铺子后面的小屋里挪到楼上他们称作客厅的房间里去。我们每次走到窗前，都免不了看见他们。一家之主吃饱喝足之后，总是坐在安乐椅里，手帕盖在眼睛上睡觉。孩子们总是趴在窗前，假装读书，实则张望着底下的人。四点钟左右，他们的爸爸一般就醒了，吩咐在钢琴上连弹一曲又一曲的赞美诗。天气允许时，下面那扇窗会推开一点，让邻居们也能享用一曲《生命之火》（Vital Spark）的演奏；那位父亲还不时“加入进来”，在和声大致有把握的曲末，补上一两个低音。五点钟，即将到来的茶点的前景，把我们从观赏那片风景——或者说砖景——的沉思中解救出来些许。我说“些许”，因为麦凯家的饭桌上总有几分不痛快。他的妻子是个诚实善良的小妇人，可她太依恋他、太仰赖他，以至于成了他单纯的回声。她没有一条不是他的意见；每逢他说到超出家常琐事的话头，她便费劲地听着，回应通常是把麦凯自己的议论有气无力地重复一遍。于是他面前永远摆着一个衰弱版的自己，这令他大为恼火，尽管他爱她；因为这样一个女人，谁又能不爱呢——只要他一声吩咐，她会毫不迟疑地割开自己的血管，为他放尽体内的最后一滴血。我一再听见他对某人某事发一通议论，接着照例响起一声赞同的附和，把他激得火起，立刻又苦涩地把自己的话推翻，弄得可怜的麦凯太太一头雾水。这样一箭射来，她往往吓得不敢作声。照例，凡容易显出妻子依附于他的话题，他在家里一概不鼓励。他故意只谈她那些家务，问她补没补他的衣裳、订没订煤。她知道这些根本不是他心里想的事，便用绝望的声调回答，那声调显出她多么深切地感到自己被硬生生地屈就到了那个层次。我非常怜悯她；有时，眼看她与自己天生的限度苦苦挣扎，我几乎情难自禁，只好起身走开。她像孩子一样多情。麦凯进屋来，若恰好走到她跟前吻她一下，她的脸顿时绽放出最甜美、最幸福的笑。我记得有一回，他对她发了一通异乎寻常的脾气，出门前却又悔了，双手捧住她的头，把嘴唇印了上去。我看见她轻轻把那只手从额前取下来，贴到自己的嘴上，泪水正顺着面颊往下淌。无论怎样，她绝不肯说丈夫一句不是。麦凯有时暴烈，有时不公。他恨自己的职业，那股按捺不住的憎恶常常不分青红皂白地发泄到身边的一切上。他的孩子们常觉得他近乎蛮横，其实他在那种心境里根本就没有见他们。真正萦绕在他心头、排开其余一切的，是某种格外令他憎厌之事的刺痛，而孩子们对此一无所知。孩子们背地里向麦凯太太抱怨时，她总是一句话：“他心里烦啊。”而且她总不免细数他们的过错，说正是这些过错给了他动怒的由头。

===Original===

I think M’Kay’s treatment of her wholly wrong. I think that he ought not to have imposed himself upon her so imperiously. I think he ought to have striven to ascertain what lay concealed in that modest heart, to have encouraged its expression and development, to have debased himself before her that she might receive courage to rise, and he would have found that she had something which he had not; not *his* something perhaps, but something which would have made his life happier. As it was, he stood upon his own ground above her. If she could reach him, well and good, if not, the helping hand was not proffered, and she fell back, hopeless. Later on he discovered his mistake. She became ill very gradually, and M’Kay began to see in the distance a prospect of losing her. A frightful pit came in view. He became aware that he could not do without her. He imagined what his home would have been with other women whom he knew, and he confessed that with them he would have been less contented. He acknowledged that he had been guilty of a kind of criminal epicurism; that he rejected in foolish, fatal, nay, even wicked indifference, the bread of life upon which he might have lived and thriven. His whole effort now was to suppress himself in his wife. He read to her, a thing he never did before, and when she misunderstood, he patiently explained; he took her into his counsels and asked her opinion; he abandoned his own opinion for hers, and in the presence of her children he always deferred to her, and delighted to acknowledge that she knew more than he did, that she was right and he was wrong. She was now confined to her house, and the end was near, but this was the most blessed time of her married life. She grew under the soft rain of his loving care, and opened out, not, indeed, into an oriental flower, rich in profound mystery of scent and colour, but into a blossom of the chalk-down. Altogether concealed and closed she would have remained if it had not been for this beneficent and heavenly gift poured upon her. He had just time enough to see what she really was, and then she died. There are some natures that cannot unfold under pressure or in the presence of unregarding power. Hers was one. They require a clear space round them, the removal of everything which may overmaster them, and constant delicate attention. They require too a recognition of the fact, which M’Kay for a long time did not recognise, that it is folly to force them and to demand of them that they shall be what they cannot be. I stood by the grave this morning of my poor, pale, clinging little friend now for some years at peace, and I thought that the tragedy of Promethean torture or Christ-like crucifixion may indeed be tremendous, but there is a tragedy too in the existence of a soul like hers, conscious of its feebleness and ever striving to overpass it, ever aware that it is an obstacle to the return of the affection of the man whom she loves.

===Chinese===

我认为麦凯对她的做法全然错了。我想，他本不该那样专横地把自己强加于她。我想，他本该努力探明那颗谦抑的心里藏着什么，鼓励它表达、促它成长，本该在她面前放低自己，好让她得到抬起头来的勇气。那样他就会发现，她拥有他所没有的东西——或许不是*他那种*东西，却是能让他的生活更幸福的东西。而实际上，他站在高处自己的地盘上。她够得着他，那自然好；够不着，那只援手也不伸下来，她便退回去，绝望了。后来他发现了自己的错。她的病是慢慢重起来的，麦凯开始远远望见失去她的前景。一道可怕的深渊显现在眼前。他意识到自己少不了她。他设想若换作他认识的其他女人，他的家会是什么样子；他承认，同她们在一起，他不会这样满足。他承认自己犯了一种罪过的享乐主义：他以愚蠢的、致命的、甚至可谓邪恶的冷漠，拒绝了那本可以让他赖以存活、兴旺的生命之粮。如今他全部的努力，是在妻子面前克制自己。他给她读书——这是他从前从未做过的事——她听不懂时，他耐心讲解；他让她参与商量，征求她的意见；他舍弃己见而从她之见；当着孩子们的面，他总是敬让她三分，还乐于承认她懂得比他多，她对，他错。她这时已出不了门，大限将近，但这却是她婚后最有福的一段时光。在他爱怜的柔雨里，她生长、绽放了——诚然，不是开成一朵色香浓艳、深蕴神秘的东方之花，而是开成一朵白垩山丘上的小花。他刚刚来得及看清她真正的样子，她便去了。有些天性，在重压之下、在漠然不顾的威权面前，是绽放不开的。她的天性便是如此。这样的天性需要四周留出空地，需要挪开一切可能压倒它们的东西，需要持续而细腻的照拂。它们还需要人们承认一个麦凯长久不肯承认的事实：逼迫它们、强要它们成为它们成不了的样子，是愚蠢的。今天早晨，我站在我那可怜、苍白、依人小鸟般的小朋友墓前——她已安息数年了。我想，普罗米修斯式的酷刑或基督式的钉十字架固然可能是惊天动地的悲剧，但像她这样一个灵魂的存在，也是一场悲剧：它自觉软弱，又总想越过这软弱，又总意识到自己正是所爱之人回心转意的障碍。

===Original===

Meals, as I have said, were disagreeable at M’Kay’s, and when we wanted to talk we went out of doors. The evening after our visit to the debating hall we moved towards Portland Place, and walked up and down there for an hour or more. M’Kay had a passionate desire to reform the world. The spectacle of the misery of London, and of the distracted swaying hither and thither of the multitudes who inhabit it, tormented him incessantly. He always chafed at it, and he never seemed sure that he had a right to the enjoyment of the simplest pleasures so long as London was before him. What a farce, he would cry, is all this poetry, philosophy, art, and culture, when millions of wretched mortals are doomed to the eternal darkness and crime of the city! Here are the educated classes occupying themselves with exquisite emotions, with speculations upon the Infinite, with addresses to flowers, with the worship of waterfalls and flying clouds, and with the incessant portraiture of a thousand moods and variations of love, while their neighbours lie grovelling in the mire, and never know anything more of life or its duties than is afforded them by a police report in a bit of newspaper picked out of the kennel. We went one evening to hear a great violin-player, who played such music, and so exquisitely, that the limits of life were removed. But we had to walk up the Haymarket home, between eleven and twelve o’clock, and the violin-playing became the merest trifling. M’Kay had been brought up upon the Bible. He had before him, not only there, but in the history of all great religious movements, a record of the improvement of the human race, or of large portions of it, not merely by gradual civilisation, but by inspiration spreading itself suddenly. He could not get it out of his head that something of this kind is possible again in our time. He longed to try for himself in his own poor way in one of the slums about Drury Lane. I sympathised with him, but I asked him what he had to say. I remember telling him that I had been into St. Paul’s Cathedral, and that I pictured to myself the cathedral full, and myself in the pulpit. I was excited while imagining the opportunity offered me of delivering some message to three or four thousand persons in such a building, but in a minute or two I discovered that my sermon would be very nearly as follows: “Dear friends, I know no more than you know; we had better go home.” I admitted to him that if he could believe in hellfire, or if he could proclaim the Second Advent, as Paul did to the Thessalonians, and get people to believe, he might change their manners, but otherwise he could do nothing but resort to a much slower process. With the departure of a belief in the supernatural departs once and forever the chance of regenerating the race except by the school and by science.2 However, M’Kay thought he would try. His earnestness was rather a hindrance than a help to him, for it prevented his putting certain important questions to himself, or at any rate it prevented his waiting for distinct answers. He recurred to the apostles and Bunyan, and was convinced that it was possible even now to touch depraved men and women with an idea which should recast their lives. So it is that the main obstacle to our success is a success which has preceded us. We instinctively follow the antecedent form, and consequently we either pass by, or deny altogether, the life of our own time, because its expression has changed. We never do practically believe that the Messiah is not incarnated twice in the same flesh. He came as Jesus, and we look for Him as Jesus now, overlooking the manifestation of today, and dying, perhaps, without recognising it.

===Chinese===

如前所说，麦凯家的饭桌上有几分不痛快；想要谈话时，我们就到户外去。去辩论厅的那个晚上过后，我们朝波特兰广场（Portland Place）走去，在那儿来回踱了一个多钟头。麦凯怀着一腔改造世界的热望。伦敦的惨状，以及栖居其中的芸芸众生惶惑摇摆、东倒西歪的景象，无时无刻不折磨着他。他为此焦躁不已；只要伦敦还在眼前，他仿佛连享受最简单的快乐都不安理得。他会喊道：千百万可怜人注定沉沦在这城市永恒的黑暗与罪恶里，这时候还谈什么诗歌、哲学、艺术、教养，全是闹剧！这边，受教育阶级忙着咀嚼精致的情感，玄思无限，对花吟咏，礼赞飞瀑流云，没完没了地描摹爱情的一千种心绪与变奏；那边，他们的邻人趴在泥里，关于生活及其义务，他们所知道的一切，不比从街沟里捡来的一角报纸上警局纪事所给的更多。有天晚上，我们去听一位伟大的小提琴家演奏；那音乐、那精湛的技艺，简直把生命的疆界都消除了。可是十一点到十二点之间，我们还得上干草市场（Haymarket）走回家去，那琴声便顿成区区小事。麦凯是读着《圣经》长大的。摆在他面前的——不仅在《圣经》里，也在一切伟大宗教运动的历史里——是一份人类的记录，或者说人类很大一部分的记录：他们得以改善，不只靠渐进的文明，也靠忽然传开的灵感。他忘不了这种事在我们这个时代也可能再来一回。他渴望在德鲁里巷（Drury Lane）一带的某个贫民窟里，以他自己微薄的方式试上一试。我同情他，但我问他，他要讲些什么。记得我对他说过，我进过圣保罗大教堂（St. Paul’s Cathedral）；我设想教堂坐得满满的，而我自己站在讲道坛上。想象着在这样一座建筑里向三四千人传讲某种信息的机会，我一时兴奋；可不到一两分钟我就发现，我的讲道差不多只能是这样：“亲爱的朋友们，我所知道的并不比你们多；咱们还是回家吧。”我向他承认：倘若他能信地狱之火，或者能像保罗对帖撒罗尼迦人那样宣告基督再来，并且让人们相信，那他或许能改变他们的行止；不然的话，他只能走一条慢得多的路。随着对超自然之信仰的离去，不经学校与科学而使人类重生的机会，便一去不返了。2然而麦凯还是想试一试。他的热诚于他与其说是助力，不如说是障碍，因为它妨碍他向自己提出某些要紧的问题，或者至少妨碍他等个明白的答案。他一再想到众使徒和班扬，坚信即便在今日，也仍可能用一个观念去触动堕落的男女，使他们的生命焕然一新。原来，我们成功的主要障碍，是先行于我们的那些成功。我们本能地追摹前人的样式，于是对我们自己时代的生活，因其表达变了样，我们不是失之交臂，就是干脆否认。弥赛亚并不会以同一副肉身两次降生——这个道理，我们实际上从不相信。他以耶稣的身份来过；如今我们仍指望他再作为耶稣来，却把今日的显现看漏了，说不定到死也不曾认出它。

===Original===

M’Kay had found a room near Parker Street, Drury Lane, in which he proposed to begin, and that night, as we trod the pavement of Portland Place, he propounded his plans to me, I listening without much confidence, but loth nevertheless to take the office of Time upon myself, and to disprove what experience would disprove more effectually. His object was nothing less than gradually to attract Drury Lane to come and be saved.

The first Sunday I went with him to the room. As we walked over the Drury Lane gratings of the cellars a most foul stench came up, and one in particular I remember to this day. A man half dressed pushed open a broken window beneath us, just as we passed by, and there issued such a blast of corruption, made up of gases bred by filth, air breathed and rebreathed a hundred times, charged with odours of unnameable personal uncleanness and disease, that I staggered to the gutter with a qualm which I could scarcely conquer. At the doors of the houses stood grimy women with their arms folded and their hair disordered. Grimier boys and girls had tied a rope to broken railings, and were swinging on it. The common door to a score of lodgings stood ever open, and the children swarmed up and down the stairs carrying with them patches of mud every time they came in from the street. The wholesome practice which amongst the decent poor marks off at least one day in the week as a day on which there is to be a change; when there is to be some attempt to procure order and cleanliness; a day to be preceded by soap and water, by shaving, and by as many clean clothes as can be procured, was unknown here. There was no break in the uniformity of squalor; nor was it even possible for any single family to emerge amidst such altogether suppressive surroundings. All self-respect, all effort to do anything more than to satisfy somehow the grossest wants, had departed. The shops were open; most of them exhibiting a most miscellaneous collection of goods, such as bacon cut in slices, firewood, a few loaves of bread, and sweetmeats in dirty bottles. Fowls, strange to say, black as the flagstones, walked in and out of these shops, or descended into the dark areas. The undertaker had not put up his shutters. He had drawn down a yellow blind, on which was painted a picture of a suburban cemetery. Two funerals, the loftiest effort of his craft, were depicted approaching the gates. When the gas was alight behind the blind, an effect was produced which was doubtless much admired. He also displayed in his window a model coffin, a work of art. It was about a foot long, varnished, studded with little brass nails, and on the lid was fastened a rustic cross stretching from end to end. The desire to decorate existence in some way or other with more or less care is nearly universal. The most sensual and the meanest almost always manifest an indisposition to be content with mere material satisfaction. I have known selfish, gluttonous, drunken men spend their leisure moments in trimming a bed of scarlet geraniums, and the vulgarest and most commonplace of mortals considers it a necessity to put a picture in the room or an ornament on the mantelpiece. The instinct, even in its lowest forms, is divine. It is the commentary on the text that man shall not live by bread alone. It is evidence of an acknowledged compulsion﻿—of which art is the highest manifestation﻿—to *escape*. In the alleys behind Drury Lane this instinct, the very salt of life, was dead, crushed out utterly, a symptom which seemed to me ominous, and even awful to the last degree. The only house in which it survived was in that of the undertaker, who displayed the willows, the black horses, and the coffin. These may have been nothing more than an advertisement, but from the care with which the cross was elaborated, and the neatness with which it was made to resemble a natural piece of wood, I am inclined to believe that the man felt some pleasure in his work for its own sake, and that he was not utterly submerged. The cross in such dens as these, or, worse than dens, in such sewers! If it be anything, it is a symbol of victory, of power to triumph over resistance, and even death. Here was nothing but sullen subjugation, the most grovelling slavery, mitigated only by a tendency to mutiny. Here was a strength of circumstance to quell and dominate which neither Jesus nor Paul could have overcome﻿—worse a thousandfold than Scribes or Pharisees, or any form of persecution. The preaching of Jesus would have been powerless here; in fact, no known stimulus, nothing ever held up before men to stir the soul to activity, can do anything in the back streets of great cities so long as they are the cesspools which they are now.

===Chinese===

麦凯在德鲁里巷帕克街（Parker Street）附近找到了一间屋子，打算就从那里开始。那天夜里，我们踏着波特兰广场的人行道，他把计划向我摊开。我听着，信心不多，却不肯自己充任时间的角色，去否定那终将被经验更有效否定掉的东西。他的目标不小——无非是要一点点把德鲁里巷引来，让它得救。

头一个星期日，我同他一起去了那间屋子。走过德鲁里巷地下室的通风格栅时，一股恶臭冲了上来；有一股我至今记得。正走过时，一个半裸的男人在我们脚底下推开一扇破窗，涌出一阵腐臭的气流——那是污秽酿出的浊气、被呼吸过千百遍的死空气，饱含着说不出口的个人不洁与疾病的气味——我一阵翻胃，踉跄到街沟边，几乎把持不住。家门口站着满身污垢的女人，抱着胳膊，头发蓬乱。更脏的男孩女孩们把绳子拴在断栏杆上，荡来荡去。通往二三十家住户的公用大门永远敞着，孩子们在楼梯上爬上爬下，每从街上进来一趟，就带进一片泥斑。在正派的穷人中间，有一种合乎卫生的习惯：一周里至少划出一天来变一变样，设法求得一点秩序与洁净；这一天之前要用肥皂清水洗身，要刮脸，要尽可能穿上干净衣裳。这习惯，此处全然没有。穷困龌龊的千篇一律没有一丝缺口；处在这样全然压人的环境里，随便哪一家人也休想冒出头来。一切自尊，一切超出糊口苟活之外的努力，都消失了。店铺开着门；多数铺子里陈放着五花八门的杂货：切好的培根、柴火、几条面包，还有脏瓶子里的糖果。说来奇怪，几只母鸡，黑得跟石板路一样，在这些铺子里进进出出，或者下到黑黢黢的地下室前坑里。棺材店老板没有上排门。他拉下一幅黄色遮帘，上面画着一幅郊外墓园的图景。画面上是两支出殡的队伍——他手艺的最高成就——正走向园门。帘后煤气灯一亮，那效果想必大受赞叹。他还在橱窗里摆了一口模型棺材，一件艺术品。棺身约一英尺长，上了清漆，钉着小小的铜钉，棺盖上一枚质朴的十字架从头贯到尾。以或多或少的用心把生活装点一番的愿望，几乎是人人皆有的。最贪于口腹、最卑琐的人，也几乎总不肯仅以满足于物质为足。我见过自私、饕餮、酗酒的男人，把闲暇都花在侍弄一畦猩红天竺葵上；最俗不可耐、最平庸的人，也认为屋里非得挂一幅画、壁炉架上非得摆一件摆设不可。这本能，哪怕以它最低的形态存在，也是神圣的。它是对“人活着不是单靠食物”这句话的注解。它证明人承认自己受着一种强制——艺术是其最高的显现——要*逃离*。而在德鲁里巷背后的小巷里，这本能——生活真正的盐——已经死了，被碾得粉碎；在我看来，这征兆不祥，甚至可怕到了极点。唯一还存留着它的，是那家棺材店，店里陈列着柳枝、黑马和棺材。这些或许不过是广告；但从那十字架精心雕琢的功夫、从它被细致地做得像一段天然木头的用心看，我倒倾向于相信，这人从活计本身感到几分乐趣，还不曾彻底没顶。十字架，在这样的窝窟里，或者比窝窟更糟的下水道里！十字架若有什么意义，它便是胜利的象征，是战胜抵抗乃至死亡的力量的象征。而这里只有阴郁的屈服，只有最匍匐的奴役，仅有的缓和不过是随时会闹事的一点倾向。这里的环境之力，连耶稣或保罗也降服不了——比文士、法利赛人或任何形式的迫害都要糟上千倍。耶稣的宣讲在这里将无能为力；事实上，只要大城市的后街还是眼下这样的粪池，任何已知的刺激、任何曾被举到人前以激发灵魂活动的东西，在这里都无济于事。

===Original===

We came to the room. About a score of M’Kay’s own friends were there, and perhaps half-a-dozen outsiders, attracted by the notice which had been pasted on a board at the entrance. M’Kay announced his errand. The ignorance and misery of London he said were intolerable to him. He could not take any pleasure in life when he thought upon them. What could he do? that was the question. He was not a man of wealth. He could not buy up these hovels. He could not force an entrance into them and persuade their inhabitants to improve themselves. He had no talents wherewith to found a great organisation or create public opinion. He had determined, after much thought, to do what he was now doing. It was very little, but it was all he could undertake. He proposed to keep this room open as a place to which those who wished might resort at different times, and find some quietude, instruction, and what fortifying thoughts he could collect to enable men to endure their almost unendurable sufferings. He did not intend to teach theology. Anything which would be serviceable he would set forth, but in the main he intended to rely on holding up the examples of those who were greater than ourselves and were our redeemers. He meant to teach Christ in the proper sense of the word. Christ now is admired probably more than He had ever been. Everybody agrees to admire Him, but where are the people who really do what He did? There is no religion nowadays. Religion is a mere literature. Cultivated persons sit in their studies and write overflowingly about Jesus, or meet at parties and talk about Him; but He is not of much use to me unless I say to myself, how is it with thee? unless I myself become what He was. This was the meaning of Jesus to the Apostle Paul. Jesus was in him; he had put on Jesus; that is to say, Jesus lived in him like a second soul, taking the place of his own soul and directing him accordingly. That was religion, and it is absurd to say that the English nation at this moment, or any section of it, is religious. Its educated classes are inhabited by a hundred minds. We are in a state of anarchy, each of us with a different aim and shaping himself according to a different type; while the uneducated classes are entirely given over to the “natural man.” He was firmly persuaded that we need religion, poor and rich alike. We need some controlling influence to bind together our scattered energies. We do not know what we are doing. We read one book one day and another book another day, but it is idle wandering to right and left; it is not advancing on a straight road. It is not possible to bind ourselves down to a certain defined course, but still it is an enormous, an incalculable advantage for us to have some irreversible standard set up in us by which everything we meet is to be judged. That is the meaning of the prophecy﻿—whether it will ever be fulfilled God only knows﻿—that Christ shall judge the world. All religions have been this. They have said that in the midst of the infinitely possible﻿—infinitely possible evil and infinitely possible good too﻿—we become distracted. A thousand forces good and bad act upon us. It is necessary, if we are to be men, if we are to be saved, that we should be rescued from this tumult, and that our feet should be planted upon a path. His object, therefore, would be to preach Christ, as before said, and to introduce into human life His unifying influence. He would try and get them to see things with the eyes of Christ, to love with His love, to judge with His judgment. He believed Christ was fitted to occupy this place. He deliberately chose Christ as worthy to be our central, shaping force. He would try by degrees to prove this; to prove that Christ’s way of dealing with life is the best way, and so to create a genuinely Christian spirit, which, when any choice of conduct is presented to us, will prompt us to ask first of all, how would Christ have it? or, when men and things pass before us, will decide through him what we have to say about them. M’Kay added that he hoped his efforts would not be confined to talking. He trusted to be able, by means of this little meeting, gradually to gain admittance for himself and his friends into the houses of the poor and do some practical good. At present he had no organisation and no plans. He did not believe in organisation and plans preceding a clear conception of what was to be accomplished. Such, as nearly as I can now recollect, is an outline of his discourse. It was thoroughly characteristic of him. He always talked in this fashion. He was forever insisting on the aimlessness of modern life, on the powerlessness of its vague activities to mould men into anything good, to restrain them from evil or moderate their passions, and he was possessed by a vision of a new Christianity which was to take the place of the old and dead theologies. I have reported him in my own language. He strove as much as he could to make his meaning plain to everybody. Just before he finished, three or four out of the half-a-dozen outsiders who were present whistled with all their might and ran down the stairs shouting to one another. As we went out they had collected about the door, and amused themselves by pushing one another against us, and kicking an old kettle behind us and amongst us all the way up the street, so that we were covered with splashes. Mrs. M’Kay went with us, and when we reached home, she tried to say something about what she had heard. The cloud came over her husband’s face at once; he remained silent for a minute, and getting up and going to the window, observed that it ought to be cleaned, and that he could hardly see the opposite house. The poor woman looked distressed, and I was just about to come to her rescue by continuing what she had been saying, when she rose, not in anger, but in trouble, and went upstairs.

===Chinese===

我们到了那间屋子。屋里大约有二十来位麦凯自己的朋友，还有大约六个外人，是被贴在门口布告牌上的告示引来的。麦凯说明了自己的来意。他说，伦敦的无知与苦难令他无法忍受。一想到这些，他便无法从生活里得到任何乐趣。他能做什么呢？问题就在这里。他不是富人，买不下这些破屋；也不能闯进屋里，劝住户们自求上进。他没有创立庞大组织、造就舆论的才具。他经过反复思量，决定了做他眼下做的这件事。事情很小，但已是他所能承担的全部。他打算把这间屋子一直开着，让愿意来的人随时可以来，在这里寻得一点安静、一点教益，以及他所能收集到的种种给人力量的思想，好让人熬得住几乎熬不住的苦难。他不打算讲授神学。凡是有用的东西他都愿意摆出来，但主要地，他打算依靠举出那些比我们伟大、做我们救赎者的人为榜样。他要用这个词的本义来传讲基督。如今基督受仰慕的程度，或许超过以往任何时候。人人都同意仰慕他，可真正行他所行之事的人在哪里呢？如今是没有宗教的。宗教不过是一门文学。有教养的人坐在书房里，写起耶稣来下笔千言，或在聚会上高谈耶稣；可他于我并没有多大用处，除非我对自己说：你的光景如何？除非我自己成为他所是的样子。对使徒保罗来说，耶稣的意义正在于此。耶稣在他里面；他穿上了耶稣；这就是说，耶稣像第二个灵魂住在他里面，取代了他自己的灵魂，并照此引导他。那才叫宗教；而要说此时的英格兰民族、或其中任何一部分人是信教的，那是荒谬。它的受教育阶层里住着上百种心思。我们处于无政府状态，人人目标各异，各自照着不同的典型塑造自己；而未受教育的阶层则完全交给了“属血气的人”。他坚信我们需要宗教，穷人和富人皆然。我们需要某种统辖的力量，把涣散的精力束拢起来。我们不知道自己在做什么。今天读这本书，明天读那本书，那只是左顾右盼的闲逛，不是在直路上前进。要把自己钉死在一条明确的路径上，这做不到；但在我们心里立起某个不可移易的标准，凡遇事皆拿它来衡量，仍旧是一种巨大的、无可估量的裨益。那便是“基督要审判世界”这句预言的意义——它究竟会不会应验，只有神知道。一切宗教莫不如此。它们说，在无限的可能性之中——无限的可能为恶，也无限的可能为善——我们心神迷乱。千万种或善或恶的力量作用于我们。倘若我们要成人、要得救，就必须从这场喧嚣中被救出来，双脚必须踏上一条路上。因此，如前所述，他的目标便是传讲基督，把基督那统合的力量引进人的生命。他要设法让人用基督的眼睛看事物，用基督的爱去爱，用基督的判断去判断。他相信基督配得上这个位置。他郑重地选定基督，认他值得我们奉为中心的塑造之力。他要一步一步证明这一点：证明基督对待生活的方式是最好的方式，从而造出一种真正的基督教精神——每逢行为的抉择摆在我们面前，它先催我们问：基督会怎样？每逢人与事从我们眼前经过，它便借着他来断定我们当如何评说。麦凯又补充说，他希望自己的努力不致限于空谈。他指望借这个小小的聚会，逐渐为自己和朋友们敲开穷人的家门，做些切实的好处。眼下他既无组织，也无计划。他不相信在还没有想清楚要成就什么之前，就该先去搞组织和计划。就我现在所能记得的，他的讲话大致便是这个轮廓。这完全是他这个人的本色。他说话向来是这个路数。他永远在强调现代生活的漫无目标，强调那些含混的活动无力把人塑造成任何好东西，无力拦人作恶、平息情欲；他心里萦绕着一个新基督教的异象，要让它取代那些陈旧死灭的神学。我用我自己的话转述了他。他尽力让每个人都能听懂他的意思。就在他快讲完时，那六来个外人中的三四个拼命吹起口哨，叫嚷着奔下楼去。我们出门时，他们已聚在门口，彼此推搡着往我们身上撞，还把一只破水壶一路踢在我们身后、踢在我们中间，直到街尾，溅得我们满身泥水。麦凯太太也同我们去了。到家后，她想说说自己听到的东西。她丈夫脸上立刻罩上乌云；他沉默了一分钟，起身走到窗前，说窗子该擦了，对面的房子都快看不见了。可怜的女人显得很窘。我正想接过她的话头替她解围，她却站起身来——不是恼怒，而是难过——上楼去了。

## III Miss Leroy / 三 勒罗伊小姐

===Original===
During the great French war there were many French prisoners in my native town. They led a strange isolated life, for they knew nothing of our language, nor, in those days, did three people in the town understand theirs. The common soldiers amused themselves by making little trifles and selling them. I have now before me a box of coloured straw with the date 1799 on the bottom, which was bought by my grandfather. One of these prisoners was an officer named Leroy. Why he did not go back to France I never heard, but I know that before I was born he was living near our house on a small income; that he tried to teach French, and that he had as his companion a handsome daughter who grew up speaking English. What she was like when she was young I cannot say, but I have had her described to me over and over again. She had rather darkish brown hair, and she was tall and straight as an arrow. This she was, by the way, even into old age. She surprised, shocked, and attracted all the sober persons in our circle. Her ways were not their ways. She would walk out by herself on a starry night without a single companion, and cause thereby infinite talk, which would have converged to a single focus if it had not happened that she was also in the habit of walking out at four o’clock on a summer’s morning, and that in the church porch of a little village not far from us, which was her favourite resting-place, a copy of the *De Imitatione Christi* was found which belonged to her. So the talk was scattered again and its convergence prevented. She used to say doubtful things about love. One of them struck my mother with horror. Miss Leroy told a male person once, and told him to his face, that if she loved him and he loved her, and they agreed to sign one another’s foreheads with a cross as a ceremony, it would be as good to her as marriage. This may seem a trifle, but nobody now can imagine what was thought of it at the time it was spoken. My mother repeated it every now and then for fifty years. It may be conjectured how easily any other girls of our acquaintance would have been classified, and justly classified, if they had uttered such barefaced Continental immorality. Miss Leroy’s neighbours were remarkably apt at classifying their fellow-creatures. They had a few, a very few holes, into which they dropped their neighbours, and they must go into one or the other. Nothing was more distressing than a specimen which, notwithstanding all the violence which might be used to it, would not fit into a hole, but remained an exception. Some lout, I believe, reckoning on the legitimacy of his generalisation, and having heard of this and other observations accredited to Miss Leroy, ventured to be slightly rude to her. What she said to him was never known, but he was always shy afterwards of mentioning her name, and when he did he was wont to declare that she was “a rum un.” She was not particular, I have heard, about personal tidiness, and this I can well believe, for she was certainly not distinguished when I knew her for this virtue. She cared nothing for the linen-closet, the spotless bed-hangings, and the bright poker, which were the true household gods of the respectable women of those days. She would have been instantly set down as “slut,” and as having “nasty dirty forrin ways,” if a peculiar habit of hers had not unfortunately presented itself, most irritating to her critics, so anxious promptly to gratify their philosophic tendency towards scientific grouping. Mrs. Mobbs, who lived next door to her, averred that she always slept with the window open. Mrs. Mobbs, like everybody else, never opened her window except to “air the room.” Mrs. Mobbs’ best bedroom was carpeted all over, and contained a great four-post bedstead, hung round with heavy hangings, and protected at the top from draughts by a kind of firmament of white dimity. Mrs. Mobbs stuffed a sack of straw up the chimney of the fireplace, to prevent the fall of the “sutt,” as she called it. Mrs. Mobbs, if she had a visitor, gave her a hot supper, and expected her immediately afterwards to go upstairs, draw the window curtains, get into this bed, draw the bed curtains also, and wake up the next morning “bilious.” This was the proper thing to do. Miss Leroy’s sitting-room was decidedly disorderly; the chairs were dusty; “yer might write yer name on the table,” Mrs. Mobbs declared; but, nevertheless, the casement was never closed night nor day; and, moreover, Miss Leroy was believed by the strongest circumstantial evidence to wash herself all over every morning, a habit which Mrs. Mobbs thought “weakening,” and somehow connected with ethical impropriety. When Miss Leroy was married, and first as an elderly woman became known to me, she was very inconsequential in her opinions, or at least appeared so to our eyes. She must have been much more so when she was younger. In our town we were all formed upon recognised patterns, and those who possessed any one mark of the pattern, had all. The wine-merchant, for example, who went to church, eminently respectable, Tory, by no means associating with the tradesfolk who displayed their goods in the windows, knowing no “experience,” and who had never felt the outpouring of the Spirit, was a specimen of a class like him. Another class was represented by the dissenting ironmonger, deacon, presiding at prayer-meetings, strict Sabbatarian, and believer in eternal punishments; while a third was set forth by “Guffy,” whose real name was unknown, who got drunk, unloaded barges, assisted at the municipal elections, and was never once seen inside a place of worship. These patterns had existed amongst us from the dimmest antiquity, and were accepted as part of the eternal order of things; so much so, that the deacon, although he professed to be sure that nobody who had not been converted would escape the fire﻿—and the wine-merchant certainly had not been converted﻿—was very far from admitting to himself that the wine-merchant ought to be converted, or that it would be proper to try and convert him. I doubt, indeed, whether our congregation would have been happy, or would have thought any the better of him, if he had left the church. Such an event, however, could no more come within the reach of our vision than a reversal of the current of our river. It would have broken up our foundations and party-walls, and would have been considered as ominous, and anything but a subject for thankfulness. But Miss Leroy was not the wine-merchant, nor the ironmonger, nor Guffy, and even now I cannot trace the hidden centre of union from which sprang so much that was apparently irreconcilable. She was a person whom nobody could have created in writing a novel, because she was so inconsistent. As I have said before, she studied Thomas à Kempis, and her little French Bible was brown with constant use. But then she read much fiction in which there were scenes which would have made our hair stand on end. The only thing she constantly abhorred in books was what was dull and opaque. Yet, as we shall see presently, her dislike to dullness, once at least in her life, notably failed her. She was not Catholic, and professed herself Protestant, but such a Protestantism! She had no sceptical doubts. She believed implicitly that the Bible was the Word of God, and that everything in it was true, but her interpretation of it was of the strangest kind. Almost all our great doctrines seemed shrunk to nothing in her eyes, while others, which were nothing to us, were all-important to her. The atonement, for instance, I never heard her mention, but Unitarianism was hateful to her, and Jesus was her God in every sense of the word. On the other hand, she was partly Pagan, for she knew very little of that consideration for the feeble, and even for the foolish, which is the glory of Christianity. She was rude to foolish people, and she instinctively kept out of the way of all disease and weakness, so that in this respect she was far below the commonplace tradesman’s wife, who visited the sick, sat up with them, and, in fact, never seemed so completely in her element as when she could be with anybody who was ill in bed.

===Chinese===
在对法大战期间，我的家乡小镇上关押着许多法国战俘。他们过着一种与世隔绝的古怪生活，因为他们不懂我们的语言，而在那个年月，全镇懂他们语言的人也凑不出三个。普通士兵靠制作小玩意儿卖钱自娱。我如今手头就有一只彩色麦秆编成的小盒，盒底写着 1799 年，是我祖父当年买下的。这些战俘中有一位军官，名叫勒罗伊（Leroy）。他为什么没有回法国，我从未听说；我只知道在我出生之前，他就靠一小笔进项住在我们家附近；他试过教法语，身边带着一个眉眼俊俏的女儿，这女儿是说英语长大的。她年轻时的模样我说不上来，但人们向我描述过一遍又一遍。她有一头深棕色的头发，身量高挑挺拔，像一支箭。顺便说一句，直到老年她依然如此。她使我们圈子里所有持重之士又吃惊、又骇然、又被吸引。她的做派不是他们的做派。她会在满天星斗的夜里独自出门散步，不带一个伴儿，由此招来无穷的话柄；若不是她偏偏又惯于在夏日清晨四点钟出门，而且人们在她最喜欢的歇脚处——离我们不远的一个小村庄的教堂门廊里——发现了一本属于她的《效法基督》（De Imitatione Christi），这些闲话本会汇聚到同一个焦点上。于是话柄又被驱散，再也聚不拢了。她惯常说些关于爱情的出格话。其中一句让我母亲惊骇不已。勒罗伊小姐有一次对一位男士说——而且是当面说——倘若她爱他、他也爱她，两人约定在彼此额头上画一个十字作为仪式，那么在她看来，这就同结婚一样作数。这在今天看来也许是桩小事，可如今没有人能想象这话当初出口时被当成了什么。我母亲此后五十年间时不时就要把它念叨一遍。可以想见，倘若我们认识的任何别的姑娘说出这种不要脸的欧陆式伤风败俗之论，人们会多么轻而易举地给她们归类——而且归得确凿有据。勒罗伊小姐的邻居们给同类分门别类的本事出奇地高。他们备有几个——极少的几个——洞穴，把邻居逐一丢进去，而且非进这个洞或那个洞不可。最教人苦恼的，莫过于一个任凭你施以何等暴力也塞不进任何洞穴、偏偏要做一个例外的标本。我相信，有个乡野粗汉，仗着自己归纳推理的天经地义，听说了这句以及另外几句归于勒罗伊小姐名下的大胆言论，便斗胆对她稍稍失礼了一回。她对他说了什么，从来无人知晓，但从那以后他提到她的名字总是发怵，偶尔提起，也总要宣称她是个“怪家伙”。听说她不大讲究个人整洁，这一点我很信得过，因为就我认识她的年月而论，她在这桩美德上确实算不得出众。亚麻布橱柜、一尘不染的床帐、擦得锃亮的火棍，这些那个年代体面妇女们真正的家宅神明，她一概不放在心上。倘若不是她偏偏有这么一个习惯——令她的批评者们大为光火，他们正急不可耐地要满足自己那科学归类的哲学癖好——她准会被当即论定为“懒婆娘”，说她有一副“又脏又贱的外国派头”。住在她隔壁的莫布斯太太（Mrs. Mobbs）断言，她睡觉总是开着窗。莫布斯太太同所有人一样，开窗只为“给房间透透气”。莫布斯太太家最好的卧室满铺地毯，摆着一张巨大的四柱床，四围垂着重重的帐幔，顶上还罩着一层白条纹布的“苍穹”挡风。莫布斯太太往壁炉烟囱里塞了一麻袋稻草，为的是防止她口中的“烟炱”落下来。莫布斯太太若有客来访，便请她吃一顿热腾腾的晚饭，随即指望她上楼去，拉上窗帘，钻进那张床，再拉上床帐，第二天早晨醒来便“胆汁失调”。这才是规矩做法。勒罗伊小姐的起居室确实凌乱；椅子上落着灰；“桌上的灰厚得能写下你的名字”，莫布斯太太宣称；然而那扇窗无论白天黑夜从不关闭；更有甚者，有最确凿的旁证表明，勒罗伊小姐每天早晨都要从头到脚洗一个全身，这个习惯在莫布斯太太看来“伤元气”，而且不知怎么总同道德上的不检点脱不了干系。勒罗伊小姐结婚之后——我初次认识她时她已是位老妇——她的见解非常不合轨辙，至少在我们眼里如此。她年轻时想必更是如此。在我们镇上，人人都是照着公认的模子铸出来的，凡是具备模子上任何一项特征的人，便样样俱全。譬如那位上教堂的酒商，极体面，托利党人，绝不与把货色陈列在橱窗里的商贩往来，不知什么叫“属灵经历”，也从未感受过圣灵的浇灌——他便是他那一类的一个标本。另一类由那位非国教派的五金商代表：执事，主持祷告会，严守安息日，相信永刑；第三类则由“加菲”充当，此人真名无人知晓，酗酒，卸驳船的货，市政选举时去帮忙，从未有人见他踏进过礼拜场所一步。这些模子自邃古以来就存在于我们中间，被当作永恒秩序的一部分接受下来；以至于那位执事，尽管自称确信凡未皈依者绝逃不脱火狱——而那位酒商分明未曾皈依——却万万不肯对自己承认，酒商应当被劝化，或者设法劝化他乃是一件得体的事。说实话，我怀疑倘若酒商真的脱离了国教会，我们的会众会不会高兴，会不会因此高看他一眼。然而这样的事，就像我们那条河倒流一样，根本进不了我们的视野。它会震塌我们的地基和隔墙，会被视为凶兆，而绝非感恩的事由。但勒罗伊小姐既非酒商，非五金商，亦非加菲，直到今天我也寻不出那个隐秘的枢纽，那么多看上去势不两立的东西正是从那里生发出来的。她是那种写小说的人无论如何也造不出来的人物，因为她太自相矛盾。正如我前面说过的，她研读托马斯·厄·肯培（Thomas à Kempis），她那本小小的法文圣经因时时翻用而磨成了褐色。可她同时又读大量小说，里面有些场景会叫我们毛发倒竖。她在书里一贯唯一憎恶的东西，唯有沉闷与晦涩。然而，正如我们随后将看到的，她对乏味的厌恶，一生中至少有一次让她大大失手。她不是天主教徒，自称新教徒，可那是怎样一种新教啊！她没有怀疑派的疑虑。她全然相信圣经是上帝的话语，里面句句属实，但她对圣经的解释却奇得出格。我们几乎所有的大教义在她眼里仿佛都缩成了乌有，而另一些在我们看来无足轻重的东西，对她却重于一切。譬如赎罪论，我从未听她提起；可一位论派令她憎恶，而耶稣在她看来是彻头彻尾的上帝。另一方面，她又有一半是异教徒，因为她几乎全无那种对软弱者、甚至对愚拙者的体恤——而那正是基督教的荣光。她对愚蠢的人出言粗鲁，又本能地回避一切疾病与羸弱，因此在这一点上，她远远不及寻常商人的妻子——那做妻子的探望病人，彻夜守候，而且老实说，每逢能陪着一个卧病在床的人，她便再没有那样如鱼得水的时候了。

===Original===
Miss Leroy’s father was republican, and so was my grandfather. My grandfather and old Leroy were the only people in our town who refused to illuminate when a victory was gained over the French. Leroy’s windows were spared on the ground that he was not a Briton, but the mob endeavoured to show my grandfather the folly of his belief in democracy by smashing every pane of glass in front of his house with stones. This drew him and Leroy together, and the result was, that although Leroy himself never set foot inside any chapel or church, Miss Leroy was often induced to attend our meetinghouse in company with a maiden aunt of mine, who rather “took to her.” Now comes the forever mysterious passage in history. There was amongst the attendants at that meetinghouse a young man who was apprentice to a miller. He was a big, soft, quiet, plump-faced, awkward youth, very good, but nothing more. He wore on Sunday a complete suit of light pepper-and-salt clothes, and continued to wear pepper-and-salt on Sunday all his life. He taught in the Sunday-school, and afterwards, as he got older, he was encouraged to open his lips at a prayer-meeting, and to “take the service” in the village chapels on Sunday evening.
