大约两年光景，麦凯（M’Kay）和我继续在德鲁里巷一带劳作。有句谚语说，成就任何事情，最难的是第一步；这谚语又被人改过，说艰难大半在最后一步。于我而言，难的既非第一步，也非最后一步，而是其间的路途。第一步通常有新开端的兴奋与指望帮衬，最后一步有凯旋在望撑持；中间的道路却无人以热忱相助，我们最容易在此处昏厥。麦凯却不屈不挠地坚持下来，支撑着我——否则我也许早已受不住绝望的诱惑——两年期满，我们仍坚守在岗位上。然而我们学到了一点东西。我们学到：在德鲁里巷本区，我们休想留下丝毫印痕。偶尔有个闲人，有时十来个，荡进来坐坐，但我们所说的对他们全然陌生；他们离开了自己的世界，彻底得仿佛身处另一个星球，我们想借朴素的言辞、借谈论我们以为他们会有兴趣的事物去接近他们，全都归于失败。直到与他们实际接触，我才知道大都市底层各阶层同上层之间相隔多远；才明白寻常人身上起作用的动机，对他们如何全然无效；才明白他们陷得多么深，深到阳光星光都照不进，浸沉在为生存无休止的挣扎、对社会无休止的战事中所自然滋生的自私自利里。有一个可怕的念头，在那些礼拜天始终盘踞着我，平日也纠缠不去：男人、女人和孩子竟生活在这样禽兽般的卑贱里，而且他们死去之后，还会有别人顶上来。我们的文明仿佛只是一层薄膜、一层薄壳，覆在火山口上，我常常疑心，有朝一日那坑会不会破壳而出，把我们全都毁掉。大城镇对于那一片片黑暗、不通、地底下的痞气的滋生与维系，难辞其咎——我们对这痞气已经领教过了。天空的污浊阴沉，街衢的肮脏，新鲜空气的缺席，穷人被圈挤在善心人无从敞开的巨大街区里，这些都是腐败的可怕动力，其活跃之烈，令人一想到倘若人口聚积不加遏止、我们的未来将如何，便不寒而栗。与不可解者面对面并不好受。一个人宁肯做任何事，也不肯承认它超出自己能力之外。他会编造悦人的虚构，幻想这里那里有一条可能的生路，但德鲁里巷这个问题浑圆坚硬，像一颗金刚石弹丸。我唯一能做的，是微弱地——我几乎要说愚蠢地——希望：某一种我们现在尚无所知的力量的存在，有朝一日会显现，能够抵御并除去那股压力；那股压力年年只在这地球的一隅，便把千百万人的灵魂扫进、压进一座与上层大气隔绝封死的地狱。

===Original===
M’Kay’s dreams therefore were not realised, and yet it would be a mistake to say that they ended in nothing. It often happens that a grand attempt, although it may fail﻿—miserably fail﻿—is fruitful in the end and leaves a result, not the hoped for result it is true, but one which would never have been attained without it. A youth strives after the impossible, and he is apt to break his heart because he has never even touched it, but nevertheless his whole life is the sweeter for the striving; and the archer who aims at a mark a hundred yards away will send his arrow further than he who sets his bow and his arm for fifty yards. So it was with M’Kay. He did not convert Drury Lane, but he saved two or three. One man whom we came to know was a labourer in Somerset House, a kind of coal porter employed in carrying coals into the offices there from the cellars below, and in other menial duties. He had about fifteen or sixteen shillings a week, and as the coals must necessarily be in the different rooms before ten o’clock in the morning, he began work early, and was obliged to live within an easy distance of the Strand. This man had originally been a small tradesman in a country town. He was honest, but he never could or never would push his trade in any way. He was fond of all kinds of little mechanical contrivings, disliked his shop, and ought to have been a carpenter or cabinetmaker﻿—not as a master but as a journeyman, for he had no ability whatever to control men or direct large operations. He was married, and a sense of duty to his wife﻿—he fortunately had no children﻿—induced him to stand or sit behind his counter with regularity, but people would not come to buy of him, because he never seemed to consider their buying as any favour conferred on him; and thus he became gradually displaced by his more energetic or more obsequious rivals. In the end he was obliged to put up his shutters. Unhappily for him, he had never been a very ardent attendant at any of the places of religious worship in the town, and he had therefore no organisation to help him. Not being master of any craft, he was in a pitiable plight, and was slowly sinking, when he applied to the solicitor of the political party for which he had always voted to assist him. The solicitor applied to the member, and the member, much regretting the difficulty of obtaining places for grown-up men, and explaining the pressure upon the Treasury, wrote to say that the only post at his disposal was that of labourer. He would have liked to offer a messengership, but the Treasury had hundreds of applications from great people who wished to dispose of favourite footmen whose services they no longer required. Our friend Taylor had by this time been brought very low, or he would have held out for something better, but there was nothing to be done. He was starving, and he therefore accepted; came to London; got a room, one room only, near Clare Market, and began his new duties. He was able to pick up a shilling or two more weekly by going on errands for the clerks during his slack time in the day, so that altogether on the average he made up about eighteen shillings. Wandering about the Clare Market region on Sunday he found us out, came in, and remained constant. Naturally, as we had so few adherents, we gradually knew these few very intimately, and Taylor would often spend a holiday or part of the Sunday with us. He was not eminent for anything in particular, and an educated man, selecting as his friends those only who stand for something, would not have taken the slightest notice of him. He had read nothing particular, and thought nothing particular﻿—he was indeed one of the masses﻿—but in this respect different, that he had not the tendency to association, aggregation, or clanship which belong to the masses generally. He was different, of course, in all his ways from his neighbours born and bred to Clare Market and its alleys. Although commonplace, he had demands made upon him for an endurance by no means commonplace, and he had sorrows which were as exquisite as those of his betters. He did not much resent his poverty. To that I think he would have submitted, and in fact he did submit to it cheerfully. What rankled in him was the brutal disregard of him at the office. He was a servant of servants. The messengers, who themselves were exposed to all the petty tyrannies of the clerks, and dared not reply, were Taylor’s masters, and sought a compensation for their own serfdom by making his ten times worse. The head messenger, who had been a butler, swore at him, and if Taylor had “answered” he would have been reported. He had never been a person of much importance, but at least he had been independent, and it was a new experience for him to feel that he was a thing fit for nothing but to be cuffed and cursed. Upon this point he used to get eloquent﻿—as eloquent as he could be, for he had small power of expression, and he would describe to me the despair which came over him down in those dark vaults at the prospect of life continuing after this fashion, and with not the minutest gleam of light even at the very end. Nobody ever cared to know the most ordinary facts about him. Nobody inquired whether he was married or single; nobody troubled himself when he was ill. If he was away, his pay was stopped; and when he returned to work nobody asked if he was better. Who can wonder that at first, when he was an utter stranger in a strange land, he was overcome by the situation, and that the world was to him a dungeon worse than that of Chillon? Who can wonder that he was becoming reckless? A little more of such a life would have transformed him into a brute. He had not the ability to become revolutionary, or it would have made him a conspirator. Suffering of any kind is hard to bear, but the suffering which especially damages character is that which is caused by the neglect or oppression of man. At any rate it was so in Taylor’s case. I believe that he would have been patient under any inevitable ordinance of nature, but he could not lie still under contempt, the knowledge that to those about him he was of less consequence than the mud under their feet. He was timid and, after his failure as a shopkeeper, and the near approach to the workhouse, he dreaded above everything being again cast adrift. Strange conflict arose in him, for the insults to which he was exposed drove him almost to madness; and yet the dread of dismissal in a moment checked him when he was about to “fire up,” as he called it, and reduced him to a silence which was torture. Once he was ordered to bring some coals for the messenger’s lobby. The man who gave him the order, finding that he was a long time bringing them, went to the top of the stairs, and bawled after him with an oath to make haste. The reason of the delay was that Taylor had two loads to bring up﻿—one for somebody else. When he got to the top of the steps, the messenger with another oath took the coals, and saying that he “would teach him to skulk there again,” kicked the other coal-scuttle down to the bottom. Taylor himself told me this; and yet, although he would have rejoiced if the man had dropped down dead, and would willingly have shot him, he was dumb. The check operated in an instant. He saw himself without a penny, and in the streets. He went down into the cellar, and raged and wept for an hour. Had he been a workman, he would probably have throttled his enemy, or tried to do it, or what is more likely, his enemy would not have dared to treat him in such fashion, but he was powerless, and once losing his situation he would have sunk down into the gutter, whence he would have been swept by the parish into the indiscriminate heap of London pauperism, and carted away to the Union, a conclusion which was worse to him than being hung.

===Chinese===
麦凯的梦想因此未能实现，然而若说它们一无所成，那就错了。伟大的尝试纵然失败——悲惨地失败——往往终究结出果实，留下一个结果，诚然不是原先指望的那个，却是没有它便永远达不到的一个。年轻人追求不可能之事，往往因从未触及它而心碎，但他的一生却因这追求而更甘美；瞄准百步之外目标的弓手，比起只把弓和臂力对准五十步的人，箭总要射得更远。麦凯正是如此。他没有感化德鲁里巷，却救下了两三个人。我们结识的一个人是萨默塞特府（Somerset House）里的劳工，一种运煤的脚夫，负责把煤从下面的地窖搬进各办公室，另带些杂役活计。他每周约有十五六先令，由于煤必须在上午十点前送进各个房间，他上工很早，只好住在离斯特兰德街不远处。这人原是外省小镇上的一个小商人。他诚实，却从来不能、也从来不肯设法招揽生意。他喜欢种种小小的机械巧思，厌恶自己的铺子，本该做个木匠或细木匠——不是做师傅，而是做雇工，因为他全无驾驭他人、指挥大场面营作的本事。他结了婚，对妻子的责任感——所幸他没有孩子——促使他规规矩矩地站柜台、坐柜台，可人们偏不来买他的东西，因为他从不把顾客的光顾当作给他的什么恩惠；于是他渐渐被精力更旺或更会奉承的同行挤垮了。到头来他只得关门歇业。不幸的是，他一向不大热忱地上镇里任何礼拜场所，因此也没有任何团体来帮扶他。他无一技之长在手，境况凄惨，正缓缓下沉，这时他向他一直投票拥护的那个政党的律师求助。律师去找那位议员，议员深表遗憾，说替成年人谋职位何其困难，又说明财政部如何如何吃紧，随后写信说，他手里唯一可给的差事就是劳工。他本想给一个信差的位置，可财政部收到的申请成百上千，都是大人物们想打发掉用不着的宠仆。我们的朋友泰勒（Taylor）这时已被逼得很低，否则他原会撑着等更好的，可是没有法子。他在挨饿，于是接受了；来到伦敦；在克莱尔市场（Clare Market）附近赁了一间房——只有一间——开始他的新差事。白天闲时他替文书们跑腿，每星期又能多挣一两个先令，合计起来平均约摸十八先令。礼拜天他在克莱尔市场一带闲逛，发现了我们，走了进来，从此再未离开。自然，由于拥护者寥寥，我们渐渐把这寥寥几人了解得极深，泰勒常同我们一起过一个假日或半个礼拜天。他没有任何出类拔萃之处，一个受教育的人若只择“有些名堂”的人为友，是断断不会看他一眼的。他没读过什么特别的书，也没想过什么特别的事——他实在是芸芸众生中的一个——只在这一点上不同：他不具备大众通常那种结帮、聚伙、拉山头的习性。当然，从做派上说，他同生在克莱尔市场及其巷弄、长在克莱尔市场及其巷弄的邻居们全都两样。他虽平庸，命运向他索取的忍耐却绝非平庸，他也有着他的悲哀，精致得不亚于任何比他高贵的人。他并不太怨恨自己的贫穷。我想贫穷这一层他是肯屈从的，事实上他也确实快快活活地屈从了。真正扎在他心头的是办公室里对他的粗暴漠视。他是仆人的仆人。那些信差自己受尽文书们种种小气的作践、不敢回嘴，却成了泰勒的主子，借把他的苦役加重十倍来补偿自己的苦役。信差头儿当过管家，开口便咒骂他，泰勒若“还嘴”，就会被上报。他从来不是什么要紧人物，但至少曾经独立自主，如今却感到自己是一件只配挨打挨骂的东西，这于他是全新的体验。一到这个题目他便滔滔不绝——尽其所能地滔滔不绝，因为他的表达能力有限——他向我描述，在那些黑地窖里，一想到日子要这样过下去、想到连尽头处也无一星最微弱的光，那种攫住他的绝望。没有人肯费心了解他最平常的境况。没有人问他已婚未婚；他病了，也没有人放在心上。他若缺勤，工钱便停发；他回来上工，也没有人问一声好些没有。谁能奇怪，起初，当他全然是异乡的异客时，他被这处境压倒，世界于他成了一座比希永的地牢更糟的地牢？谁能奇怪他渐渐变得不顾死活？这样的生活再多一点，就会把他变成一个畜生。他没有本事成为革命者，否则这生活本会把他造成一个密谋家。任何苦难都不好受，但尤其戕害性情的，是因人的漠视或压迫而生的苦难。至少泰勒的情形是如此。我相信，对于自然任何不可避免的定命，他都会忍耐；可他无法在轻蔑之下安卧——无法安于那种认知：在周围人眼里，他还不如他们脚下的泥。他胆怯，自从做店主失败、几乎进了济贫院之后，他最怕的就是再被抛向街头。他内心起了奇特的交战：他所受的侮辱几乎把他逼疯；然而对解雇的恐惧，又总在他要“发作”——他如此称呼——的一瞬把他勒住，把他压回到一种苦刑般的沉默里。有一回，他奉命给信差的门廊送煤。下命令的人见他迟迟不回，走到楼梯顶上，咒骂着朝他吼，叫他快点。耽搁的原因是泰勒要搬两趟——一趟是替别人搬的。他走到楼梯顶，那信差又咒骂一声接过煤，说“要教他再躲懒”，抬起脚把另一只煤桶踢到了底。这是泰勒亲口告诉我的；然而，尽管那人若当场倒毙他会欢天喜地、若能开枪他乐意毙了他，他还是哑然。那道闸在一瞬之间落了下来。他看见自己身无分文、流落街头。他走下地窖，狂怒地哭了一个钟头。他若是个工人，多半会掐死他的对头，或者试图去掐；更可能的是，他的对头根本不敢那样待他。可他无能为力，一旦丢了差事，他就会跌进阴沟，被教区扫进伦敦贫民那不分皂白的一堆里，装车运往联合济贫院——这样的下场，在他比绞死还难受。
===Original===
Another of our friends was a waiter in one of the public-houses and chophouses combined, of which there are so many in the Strand. He lived in a wretched alley which ran from St. Clement’s Church to Boswell Court﻿—I have forgotten its name﻿—a dark crowded passage. He was a man of about sixty﻿—invariably called John, without the addition of any surname. I knew him long before we opened our room, for I was in the habit of frequently visiting the chophouse in which he served. His hours were incredible. He began at nine o’clock in the morning with sweeping the dining-room, cleaning the tables and the gas globes, and at twelve business commenced with early luncheons. Not till three-quarters of an hour after midnight could he leave, for the house was much used by persons who supped there after the theatres. During almost the whole of this time he was on his legs, and very often he was unable to find two minutes in the day in which to get his dinner. Sundays, however, were free. John was not a head waiter, but merely a subordinate, and I never knew why at his time of life he had not risen to a better position. He used to say that “things had been against him,” and I had no right to seek for further explanations. He was married, and had had three children, of whom one only was living﻿—a boy of ten years old, whom he hoped to get into the public-house as a potboy for a beginning. Like Taylor, the world had well-nigh overpowered John entirely﻿—crushed him out of all shape, so that what he was originally, or might have been, it was almost impossible to tell. There was no particular character left in him. He may once have been this or that, but every angle now was knocked off, as it is knocked off from the rounded pebbles which for ages have been dragged up and down the beach by the waves. For a lifetime he had been exposed to all sorts of whims and caprices, generally speaking of the most unreasonable kind, and he had become so trained to take everything without remonstrance or murmuring that every cross in his life came to him as a chop alleged by an irritated customer to be raw or done to a cinder. Poor wretch! he had one trouble, however, which he could not accept with such equanimity, or rather with such indifference. His wife was a drunkard. This was an awful trial to him. The worst consequence was that his boy knew that his mother got drunk. The neighbours kindly enough volunteered to look after the little man when he was not at school, and they waylaid him and gave him dinner when his mother was intoxicated; but frequently he was the first when he returned to find out that there was nothing for him to eat, and many a time he got up at night as late as twelve o’clock, crawled downstairs, and went off to his father to tell him that “she was very bad, and he could not go to sleep.” The father, then, had to keep his son in the Strand till it was time to close, take him back, and manage in the best way he could. Over and over again was he obliged to sit by this wretched woman’s bedside till breakfast time, and then had to go to work as usual. Let anybody who has seen a case of this kind say whether the State ought not to provide for the relief of such men as John, and whether he ought not to have been able to send his wife away to some institution where she might have been tended and restrained from destroying, not merely herself, but her husband and her child. John hardly bore up under this sorrow. A man may endure much, provided he knows that he will be well supported when his day’s toil is over; but if the help for which he looks fails, he falls. Oh those weary days in that dark back dining-room, from which not a square inch of sky was visible! weary days haunted by a fear that while he was there unknown mischief was being done! weary days, whose close nevertheless he dreaded! Beaten down, baffled, disappointed, if we are in tolerable health we can contrive to live on some almost impossible chance, some most distant flicker of hope. It is astonishing how minute a crack in the heavy uniform cloud will relieve us; but when with all our searching we can see nothing, then at last we sink. Such was John’s case when I first came to know him. He attracted me rather, and bit by bit he confided his story to me. He found out that I might be trusted, and that I could sympathise, and he told me what he had never told to anybody before. I was curious to discover whether religion had done anything for him, and I put the question to him in an indirect way. His answer was that “some on ’em say there’s a better world where everything will be put right, but somehow it seemed too good to be true.” That was his reason for disbelief, and heaven had not the slightest effect on him. He found out the room, and was one of our most constant friends.

===Chinese===
我们另一位朋友是斯特兰德街一带随处可见的酒馆兼小饭馆里的侍者，那种馆子那里多得很。他住在一条从圣克莱门特教堂通往博斯韦尔院的破落小巷里——名字我忘了——一条黑暗拥挤的过道。他约摸六十岁——人人一律叫他约翰（John），不带姓。我们开设那间屋子之前我早就认识他，因为我常去他当差的那家小饭馆。他的钟点令人难以置信。早晨九点他开始打扫饭厅、擦桌子和煤气灯罩，十二点起随着早午餐开张。到午夜三刻之后他才能走，因为看完戏来吃夜宵的人多用那家馆子。这段时间里他几乎始终站着，一天里常常挤不出两分钟来吃自己的饭。礼拜天倒是空的。约翰不是侍者领班，不过是个下手，我始终不明白以他的年纪为何没有升到更好的位置。他总说“时运跟他作对”，我也就无权再追问。他结了婚，有过三个孩子，只剩一个活着——一个十岁的男孩，他指望把孩子送进酒馆当个端酒的小伙计起步。同泰勒一样，世界也几乎把约翰整个压垮——把他压得全然走了形，以致他本来是什么人、本可能是什么人，几乎无从辨认。他身上已不剩什么独特的性格。他也许曾是这样或那样的人，但如今每个棱角都被磨掉了，就像被海浪世世代代拖上拖下海滩的卵石，棱角尽失。一辈子他都在承受种种任性乖张——大抵是最不讲理的那一种——他被磨得如此驯顺，逆来顺受、一声不吭，以致生活里每一桩横逆临到他，都像一块被发火的顾客硬说夹生或烧成炭的排骨。可怜的人！然而有一样苦楚，他无法那样泰然——不如说那样麻木——地承受。他的妻子是个酒徒。这于他是一场可怕的磨难。最坏的后果是他的男孩知道母亲喝醉。邻居们好心，孩子不上学的时候自愿照看这小家伙，母亲醉了他们便拦下他、留他吃饭；可他时常是自己回家头一个发现家里没的吃，多少回他半夜爬起来——晚到十二点——摸下楼，跑到父亲那里，说“她病得很重，他睡不着”。于是做父亲的只好把儿子留在斯特兰德街直到打烊，再领他回去，尽力设法。一回又一回，他不得不坐在这苦命女人的床边直坐到早饭时分，然后照常上工。凡是见过这类情形的人，请出来说一句：国家该不该救济约翰这样的人？他该不该能把妻子送进某个机构，在那里她可以得到照看、受到约束，不至于毁掉的不只是她自己，还有她的丈夫和孩子？约翰在这场悲哀之下几乎撑不住。一个人只要知道一天苦役之后会有好好的接济，便可以承受很多；可若他指望的接济落了空，他就倒了。哦，在那间看不见一方寸天空的黑暗后厅里，那些疲乏的日子！那些被一种恐惧纠缠的日子——怕他不在家时正有着不为人知的祸事发生！那些连尽头都令他害怕的日子！被击倒、受挫、失望，只要身体还过得去，我们仍能靠某个几乎不可能的机会、某一线最遥远的希望的微光设法活下去。浓重均匀的乌云上哪怕裂开一道多细的缝，也能叫我们松一口气，这真是惊人的；可当我们搜遍四周仍一无所见，我们终于沉没。我初识约翰时，他正是这般光景。他倒颇引我注目，一点一点地，他把身世向我吐露。他发觉我可信、能体恤，便把从未向任何人说过的话告诉了我。我好奇想知道宗教可曾给过他什么，便绕着弯子问他。他回答说：“有些人说，有个更好的世界，啥事都会摆平，可不知怎么的，听着好得不像真的。”这就是他不信的缘由，天国于他没有丝毫效力。他寻到了我们那间屋子，成了我们最忠实的常客之一。

===Original===
Another friend was of a totally different type. His name was Cardinal. He was a Yorkshireman, broad-shouldered, ruddy in the face, short-necked, inclined apparently to apoplexy, and certainly to passion. He was a commercial traveller in the cloth trade, and as he had the southern counties for his district, London was his home when he was not upon his journeys. His wife was a curious contrast to him. She was dark-haired, pinched-up, thin-lipped, and always seemed as if she suffered from some chronic pain or gnawing﻿—not sufficient to make her ill, but sufficient to make her miserable. They had no children. Cardinal in early life had been a member of an orthodox Dissenting congregation, but he had fallen away. He had nobody to guide him, and the position into which he fell was peculiar. He never busied himself about religion or philosophy; indeed he had had no training which would have led him to take an interest in abstract questions, but he read all kinds of romances and poetry without any order and upon no system. He had no discriminating faculty, and mixed up together the most heterogeneous mass of trumpery novels, French translations, and the best English authors, provided only they were unworldly or sentimental. Neither did he know how far to take what he read and use it in his daily life. He often selected some fantastical motive which he had found set forth as operative in one of his heroes, and he brought it into his business, much to the astonishment of his masters and customers. For this reason he was not stable. He changed employers two or three times; and, so far as I could make out, his ground of objection to each of the firms whom he left might have been a ground of dislike in a girl to a suitor, but certainly nothing more. During the intervals of his engagements, unless he was pressed for money, he did nothing﻿—not from laziness, but because he had got a notion in his head that his mind wanted rest and reinvigoration. His habit then was to consume the whole day﻿—day after day﻿—in reading or in walking out by himself. It may easily be supposed that with a temperament like his, and with nobody near him to take him by the hand, he made great mistakes. His wife and he cared nothing for one another, but she was jealous to the last degree. I never saw such jealousy. It was strange that, although she almost hated him, she watched him with feline sharpness and patience, and would even have killed any woman whom she knew had won his affection. He, on the other hand, openly avowed that marriage without love was nothing, and flaunted without the least modification the most ideal theories as to the relation between man and woman. Not that he ever went actually wrong. His boyish education, his natural purity, and a fear never wholly suppressed, restrained him. He exasperated people by his impracticability, and it must be acknowledged that it is very irritating in a difficult complexity demanding the gravest consideration﻿—the balancing of this against that﻿—to hear a man suddenly propose some naked principle with which everybody is acquainted, and decide by it solely. I came to know him through M’Kay, who had known him for years; but M’Kay at last broke out against him, and called him a stupid fool when he threw up a handsome salary and refused to serve any longer under a house which had always treated him well, because they, moving with the times, had determined to offer their customers a cheaper description of goods, which Cardinal thought was dishonest. M’Kay said, and said truly, that many poor persons would buy these goods who could buy nothing else, and that Cardinal, before yielding to such scruples, ought to satisfy himself that, by yielding, he would not become a burden upon others less fanciful. This was just what happened. Cardinal could get no work again for a long time, and had to borrow money. I was sorry; but for my part, this and other eccentricities did not disturb my confidence in him. He was an honest, affectionate soul, and his peculiarities were a necessary result of the total chaos of a time without any moral guidance. With no church, no philosophy, no religion, the wonder is that anybody on whom use and wont relax their hold should ever do anything more than blindly rove hither and thither, arriving at nothing. Cardinal was adrift, like thousands and hundreds of thousands of others, and amidst the storm and pitchy darkness of the night, thousands and hundreds of thousands of voices offer us pilotage. It spoke well for him that he did nothing worse than take a few useless phantoms on board which did him no harm, and that he held fast to his own instinct for truth and goodness. I never let myself be annoyed by what he produced to me from his books. All that I discarded. Underneath all that was a solid worth which I loved, and which was mostly not vocal. What was vocal in him was, I am bound to say, not of much value.

===Chinese===
另一位朋友属于完全不同的类型。他姓卡迪纳尔（Cardinal）。他是个约克郡人，肩膀宽阔，面色红润，脖子短粗，看样子易犯中风，也肯定易动肝火。他是布匹行里的旅行推销员，辖区在南边几郡，不出门的日子便以伦敦为家。他的妻子同他形成奇特的对照。她黑头发，面容紧缩，嘴唇薄薄的，总像害着某种慢性的疼痛或啃噬——不至于叫她病倒，却足以叫她困苦。他们没有孩子。卡迪纳尔早年曾是正统非国教教会的一员，后来离弃了。无人引导他，他所落入的境地颇为特别。他从不为宗教或哲学费心；的确，他所受的教养也不会引他对抽象问题生趣，但他漫无次序、毫无章法地读各色传奇和诗歌。他没有鉴别力，把最杂七杂八的一堆劣质小说、法国译本和最优秀的英国作家混作一炉，只要它们超凡出尘或多愁善感。他也不知道该把读到的东西取用几分、施于日常生活。他常挑出某个异想天开的动机——那是在他某位主角身上被描述为灵验的——把它搬进生意里，叫他的东家和顾客大为错愕。因此他不安于位。他换过两三回东家；而且据我所能查明的，他离开每一家商号的理由，同一个姑娘嫌弃求婚者的理由差不多，但肯定仅此而已。两番聘用之间，若不缺钱，他便什么都不做——不是出于懒惰，而是因为他脑子里有个念头，觉得自己的心智需要休息和复元。那时他的习惯是整日整日地把光阴耗费在读书或独自出游上。可以想见，以他那样的脾性，身边又无人提携，他铸成了大错。他的妻子同他互不关心，可她嫉妒到了极点。我从没见过那样的嫉妒。奇怪的是，她虽近乎恨他，却以猫一般的尖利和耐性监视着他，凡是她知道赢得了他的情的女人，她连杀都肯。他呢，倒公开宣称没有爱情的婚姻一文不值，把关于男女关系最理想化的理论毫无收敛地四处张扬。倒不是他真的失过足。他少年时代所受的管教、他天性中的纯洁、以及一种从未全然压下去的敬畏，约束着他。他以不切实际惹恼众人，而必须承认，在一件需要最郑重权衡、需要把这头同那头细细掂量的难解纠葛里，听一个人突然抛出一个人尽皆知的光秃秃的原则、并单凭它来裁决，实在叫人光火。我是通过麦凯认识他的，麦凯与他相识多年；但麦凯终于对他发作，骂他是个蠢货——因为他扔掉一份可观的薪水，拒绝再效力于一家一向待他不薄的商号，理由只是那家商号随行就市，决意给顾客供应一种更贱的货，卡迪纳尔认为这是不诚实。麦凯说——说得也对——许多穷人会买这些货，因为他们旁的什么都买不起；卡迪纳尔在向这样的顾忌低头之前，理应先弄明白，这一低头会不会使自己成为旁人的负担——成为不如他这么多奇思异想的人的负担。后来情形正是如此。卡迪纳尔长久找不到事做，只得借钱。我很惋惜；但在我这方面，这桩以及其他种种乖僻并未动摇我对他的信任。他是个诚实的、有情有义的人，他的种种特异之处，是一个没有任何道德引导的时代的全盘混乱所必结的果实。没有教会，没有哲学，没有宗教，奇怪的不是有人随波逐流，而是习俗的缰绳一旦松开，居然还有人不是全然盲目地东游西荡、一事无成。卡迪纳尔漂泊着，像另外成千上万、几十万人一样；而在夜的狂风和漆黑里，成千上万、几十万个声音向我们兜售领航。他没有做出更糟的事，只把几个于他无害的无用幽灵请上了船，而且牢牢守着自己对真与善的本能，这已是他的光荣。他从书里搬出来给我看的东西，我从不许自己动气。那些我一概弃置。那底下的，是一种我敬爱的、大抵不发声音的扎实的价值。至于他发出来的声音，我不得不说，值不得多少。
===Original===
About the time when our room opened, Mrs. Cardinal had become almost insupportable to her husband. Poor woman; I always pitied her; she was alone sometimes for a fortnight at a stretch; she read nothing; there was no child to occupy her thoughts; she knew that her husband lived in a world into which she never entered, and she had nothing to do but to brood over imaginary infidelities. She was literally possessed, and who shall be hard upon her? Nobody cared for her; everybody with whom her husband associated disliked her, and she knew perfectly well they never asked her to their houses except for his sake. Cardinal vowed at last he would endure her no longer, and that they must separate. He was induced one Sunday morning, when his resolution was strong within him, and he was just about to give effect to it, to come with us. The quiet seemed to soothe him, and he went home with me afterwards. He was not slow to disclose to me his miserable condition, and his resolve to change it. I do not know now what I said, but it appeared to me that he ought not to change it, and that change would be for him most perilous. I thought that with a little care life might become at least bearable with his wife; that by treating her not so much as if she were criminal, but as if she were diseased, hatred might pass into pity, and pity into merciful tenderness to her, and that they might dwell together upon terms not harder than those upon which many persons who have made mistakes in youth agree to remain with each other; terms which, after much consideration, they adjudge it better to accept than to break loose, and bring upon themselves and those connected with them all that open rupture involves. The difficulty was to get Cardinal to give up his theory of what two abstract human beings should do between whom no love exists. It seemed to him something like atheism to forsake his clearly-discerned, simple rule for a course which was dictated by no easily-grasped higher law, and it was very difficult to persuade him that there is anything of equal authority in a law less rigid in its outline. However, he went home. I called on him some time afterwards, and saw that a peace, or at any rate a truce, was proclaimed, which lasted up to the day of his death. M’Kay and I agreed to make as much of Mrs. Cardinal as we could, and yielding to urgent invitation, she came to the room. This wonderfully helped to heal her. She began to feel that she was not overlooked, put on one side, or despised, and the bonds which bound her constricted lips into bitterness were loosened.

===Chinese===
我们那间屋子开张前后，卡迪纳尔太太（Mrs. Cardinal）已变得几乎令丈夫无法忍受。可怜的女人；我一向怜悯她；她有时一连半个月独自一人；她什么书也不读；没有孩子占据她的心思；她知道丈夫活在一个她从未进入的世界里，她无事可做，只能对想象中的不忠翻来覆去地冥想。她实实在在是被附了身的，谁还忍心苛责她？没有人在意她；凡是同她丈夫来往的人个个不喜欢她，她也清楚得很，他们请她进家门全是为了他的缘故。卡迪纳尔终于发誓再也受不了了，他们必须分开。一个礼拜天的早晨，他决心正炽、正要付诸实行，被我们劝动，一道同来。那份安静似乎抚慰了他，随后他同我一起回了家。他毫不迟疑地向我和盘托出自己的悲惨处境，以及改变它的决心。如今我已不记得自己当时说了什么，但我那时的看法是，他不该改变它，改变于他将极其凶险。我想，只要稍加小心，同妻子一起的生活至少可以过得下去；只要待她不像待一个罪人，而像待一个病人，恨可以化为怜，怜可以化为对她慈悲的温存，他们便可以同住下去，条件不比许多人更苛刻——那些年轻时铸过错的人，经过再三斟酌，断定接受这样的条件总比决裂为好，免得把决裂所牵连的一切公开加在自己和与自己相连的人身上。难的是叫卡迪纳尔放弃他那套理论——两个抽象的人之间若无爱情存在，应当如何如何。在他看来，丢开他那条清楚认得的简单守则，去走一条并无易于把握的更高法则指引的路，近乎无神论；要说服他相信一条轮廓不那么峻峭的法则具有同等的权威，实在困难。然而他还是回家了。过了些时日我去看他，看见一道和平——至少是休战——已经宣告，并且一直持续到他去世那天。麦凯和我商量好，要尽量善待卡迪纳尔太太，她禁不住恳请，也来了那间屋子。这奇妙地促成了她的痊愈。她开始感到自己没有被冷落、撇在一边或遭人轻视，那道把她紧闭的嘴唇勒出苦涩的绑缚，松开了。

===Original===
Another friend, and the last whom I shall name, was a young man named Clark. He was lame, and had been so from childhood. His father was a tradesman, working hard from early morning till late at night, and burdened with a number of children. The boy Richard, shut out from the companionship of his fellows, had a great love of books. When he left school his father did not know what to do with him﻿—in fact there was only one occupation open to him, and that was clerical work of one kind or another. At last he got a place in a house in Fleet Street, which did a large business in those days in sending newspapers into the country. His whole occupation all day long was to write addresses, and for this he received twenty-five shillings a week, his hours being from nine o’clock till seven. The office in which he sat was crowded, and in order to squeeze the staff into the smallest space, rent being dear, a gallery had been run round the wall about four feet from the ceiling. This was provided with desks and gas lamps, and up there Clark sat, artificial light being necessary four days out of five. He came straight from the town in which his father lived to Fleet Street, and once settled in it there seemed no chance of change for the better. He knew what his father’s struggles were; he could not go back to him, and he had not the energy to attempt to lift himself. It is very doubtful too whether he could have succeeded in achieving any improvement, whatever his energy might have been. He had got lodgings in Newcastle Street, and to these he returned in the evening, remaining there alone with his little library, and seldom moving out of doors. He was unhealthy constitutionally, and his habits contributed to make him more so. Everything which he saw which was good seemed only to sharpen the contrast between himself and his lot, and his reading was a curse to him rather than a blessing. I sometimes wished that he had never inherited any love whatever for what is usually considered to be the Best, and that he had been endowed with an organisation coarse and commonplace, like that of his colleagues. If he went into company which suited him, or read anything which interested him, it seemed as if the ten hours of the gallery in Fleet Street had been made thereby only the more insupportable, and his habitual mood was one of despondency, so that his fellow clerks who knew his tastes not unnaturally asked what was the use of them if they only made him wretched; and they were more than ever convinced that in their amusements lay true happiness. Habit, which is the saviour of most of us, the opiate which dulls the otherwise unbearable miseries of life, only served to make Clark more sensitive. The monotony of that perpetual address-copying was terrible. He has told me with a kind of shame what an effect it had upon him﻿—that sometimes for days he would feed upon the prospect of the most childish trifle because it would break in some slight degree the uniformity of his toil. For example, he would sometimes change from quill to steel pens and back again, and he found himself actually looking forward with a kind of joy﻿—merely because of the variation﻿—to the day on which he had fixed to go back to the quill after using steel. He would determine, two or three days beforehand, to get up earlier, and to walk to Fleet Street by way of Great Queen Street and Lincoln’s Inn Fields, and upon this he would subsist till the day came. He could make no longer excursions because of his lameness. All this may sound very much like simple silliness to most people, but those who have not been bound to a wheel do not know what thoughts come into the head of the strongest man who is extended on it. Clark sat side by side in his gallery with other young men of rather a degraded type, and the confinement bred in them a filthy grossness with which they tormented him. They excited in him loathsome images, from which he could not free himself either by day or night. He was peculiarly weak in his inability to cast off impressions, or to get rid of mental pictures when once formed, and his distress at being haunted by these hateful, disgusting thoughts was pitiable. They were in fact almost more than thoughts, they were transportations out of himself﻿—real visions. It would have been his salvation if he could have been a carpenter or a bricklayer, in country air, but this could not be.

===Chinese===
另一位朋友，也是我最后要提名字的一位，是个名叫克拉克（Clark）的年轻人。他跛足，自幼如此。他父亲是个商人，从清晨苦干到深夜，拖着一群儿女。这孩子理查德（Richard）被排除在同伴的交往之外，便对书生出一片深爱。他离校时，父亲不知拿他怎么办——实际上只有一条路对他敞开，那就是这样那样的文牍活计。他终于在舰队街一家商号得了个位置，那家商号当年做着一宗大生意，把报纸发往各地。他整天的活计就是写地址，为此他每周领二十五先令，钟点从九点到七点。他坐的那间办公室人满为患，为了把员工塞进最小的空间——租金太贵——沿墙在离天花板约四英尺处搭了一圈阁楼。阁楼上配了书桌和煤气灯，克拉克就坐在上面，五天里有四天需要人工照明。他从父亲住的镇上径直来到舰队街，一旦安顿下来，便再无好转的机会。他知道父亲的挣扎是怎样的；他不能回去，又没有力气设法把自己往上提。而且，无论他有多大力气，能否谋得任何改善，也很可疑。他在纽卡斯尔街赁了寓所，晚间回到那里，独自守着他小小的书库，很少出门。他体质孱弱，习惯又使他更弱。他望见的一切美好的事物，似乎只是把自己同自己的命运之间的反差磨得更锋利，他的读书于他是咒诅而非祝福。我有时希望，他压根儿没有继承对通常被视为至善之物的任何热爱，希望他生来就是一副粗粝平庸的性情，像他同事们那样。他若是进入了合意的交际，或读到什么使他感兴趣的东西，舰队街阁楼上那十个钟头似乎因此反而更难忍受，他惯常的心绪是消沉，于是知道他趣味的同事们不无道理地问：这些趣味若只会叫他痛苦，还有什么用处？他们愈发确信，真正的幸福就在他们的消遣里。习惯——它拯救我们大多数人，是麻痹人生原本不堪的种种苦楚的鸦片——在克拉克身上却只叫他更敏感。那无休无止的抄写地址的单调是可怕的。他带着一种羞惭告诉过我那活计对他的影响——有时一连好几天，他靠对一件最孩子气的小事的盼望过活，只因它多少能打破他劳作的单调。比如，他有时从鹅毛笔换成钢笔，再换回去，他发现自己竟然怀着一种欢喜——仅仅因为这点变化——盼望他定下重新用回鹅毛笔的那一天。他会提前两三天打定主意早点起床，取道大皇后街和林肯律师学院广场步行去舰队街，就靠这个念头撑到那一天来临。因为跛足，他做不了更远的出游。这一切在多数人听来也许十足像痴傻，但没有被绑上轮盘的人，不知道受轮盘之刑的最强壮的汉子，脑子里会升起什么念头。克拉克在阁楼上同几个品性颇为低下的青年并肩而坐，幽禁在他们中间滋生出一种污秽的粗鄙，他们便拿它折磨他。他们在他心里激起种种可厌的意象，白天黑夜都摆脱不掉。他特别弱的地方，在于无力抛开印象、无力抹去一经形成的心象，被这些可恨、可憎的念头纠缠不休，他的苦况令人怜悯。那些其实几乎不止是念头，而是神魂出窍的失神——真正的幻象。他若能做一名木匠或砌砖工，在乡间的空气里，那本可以救他的命，但这不可能。

===Original===
Clark had no power to think connectedly to a conclusion. When an idea came into his head, he dwelt upon it incessantly, and no correction of the false path upon which it set him was possible, because he avoided society. Work over, he was so sick of people that he went back to himself. So it came to pass that when brought into company, what he believed and cherished was frequently found to be open to obvious objection, and was often nothing better than nonsense which was rudely, and as he himself was forced to admit, justly overthrown. He ought to have been surrounded with intelligent friends, who would have enabled him to see continually the other side, and who would have prevented his long and useless wanderings. Like many other persons, too, whom I have known﻿—just in proportion to his lack of penetrative power was his tendency to occupy himself with difficult questions. By a cruel destiny he was impelled to dabble in matters for which he was totally unfitted. He never could go beyond his author a single step, and he lost himself in endless mazes. If he could but have been persuaded to content himself with sweet presentations of wholesome happy existence, with stories and with history, how much better it would have been for him! He had had no proper training whatever for anything more, he was ignorant of the exact meaning of the proper terminology of science, and an unlucky day it was for him when he picked up on a bookstall some very early translation of some German book on philosophy. One reason, as may be conjectured, for his mistakes was his education in dissenting Calvinism, a religion which is entirely metaphysical, and encourages, unhappily, in everybody a taste for tremendous problems. So long as Calvinism is unshaken, the mischief is often not obvious, because a ready solution taken on trust is provided; but when doubts arise, the evil results become apparent, and the poor helpless victim, totally at a loss, is torn first in this direction and then in the other, and cannot let these questions alone. He has been taught to believe they are connected with salvation, and he is compelled still to busy himself with them, rather than with simple external piety.

===Chinese===
克拉克没有把思考连贯地推到结论的力量。一个念头进了他的脑子，他便无休无止地盘桓其上，而这条错路的任何纠正都不可能，因为他回避人群。工一做完，他对人厌烦透顶，便退回自己。于是，每当他被带进交际场，他所信奉、所珍持的东西常被发现经不起明摆着的驳难，而且往往不过是胡话，被人粗暴地——他自己也不得不承认，公正地——推翻。他本该被聪慧的朋友们环绕，那些人能使他不断看见事情的另一面，阻拦他漫长而无益的迷途。同我认识的许多别的人一样——恰恰与他穿透力之缺乏成正比——他偏偏偏爱去钻研艰深的问题。命运的残酷驱使他去涉足他全然不适合的事情。他从来不能越出他的作者一步，迷失在无穷无尽的迷宫里。倘若能劝得他满足于健康幸福生活的甜美呈现，满足于故事和历史，那于他要好多少！他从未受过任何更高的训练，他不懂得科学术语的确切意义，而他在书摊上拾到某本德文哲学书的极早期译本的那一天，是他的倒霉日。可以想见，他的迷误的一个缘由，是他在非国教加尔文主义里所受的教育——那是一种全然形而上的宗教，不幸在人人体内培养起对惊天大问题的嗜好。只要加尔文主义根基未动，祸害往往不明显，因为有一条现成的、照单全收的答案备用；可一旦疑窦兴起，恶果便显出来，那可怜无助的受难者全然没了主意，先被朝这个方向撕，又被朝那个方向撕，却怎么也放不下这些问题。他被教导相信这些问题连着灵魂得救，于是他不得不继续纠缠于它们，而不是去修简单的外在的敬虔。
